# Hypervolume-Guided Decomposition for Parallel Expensive Multiobjective Optimization 

Liang Zhao and Qingfu Zhang, _Fellow, IEEE_ 

**_Abstract_ —The hypervolume metric is widely used to guide the search in multiobjective optimization. However, in parallel expensive multiobjective optimization, the hypervolume-based multipoint expected improvement (EI) suffers from high computational overhead and scales poorly with the batch size. To address this issue, we integrate hypervolume-based EI with the MOEA/D framework and propose a novel EI, named the expected directionbased hypervolume improvement (DirHV-EI). The DirHV-EI only measures the hypervolume improvement within each axisparallel box induced by the modified Tchebycheff scalarization. Thus, it has a simple analytical expression that can be easily computed. Theoretical analysis indicates that the maximization of our proposed improvement function can help to maximize both the weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement metrics. Using DirHV-EI, we design a decompositionbased Bayesian optimization algorithm, called DirHV-EGO, for solving expensive multiobjective optimization problems. At each iteration, the MOEA/D is used to maximize the DirHV-EI values with respect to a number of direction vectors in a collaborative manner, and a number of candidate solutions can be obtained. Then, a submodularity-based greedy selection strategy is used to select multiple query points from the candidates. Experimental results on both benchmark instances and real-world problems show that our proposed algorithm is an efficient and effective method for parallel expensive multiobjective optimization.** 

**_Index Terms_ —Decomposition, efficient global optimization, expected improvement, expensive multiobjective optimization, Hypervolume.** 

### I. INTRODUCTION 

Bayesian optimization (BO) has been widely accepted as a mainstream methodology for solving expensive black-box optimization problems in engineering design [1], [2] and machine learning [3]. At each iteration, a single-objective BO method uses some or all of the observed (i.e., evaluated) samples to build a probabilistic surrogate model, e.g., a Gaussian process (GP) model, for approximating the objective function. Then, based on the model, it optimizes an acquisition function (also known as the infill criterion) to generate one query point for true function evaluation. A few acquisition functions have been developed and studied in the context

This article has supplementary material provided by the authors and color versions of one or more figures available at http://ieeexplore.ieee.org. 

of expensive single-objective optimization [4]. Arguably, the expected improvement (EI) [5] is one of the most popular acquisition functions [6]. The EI-based BO method is also known as efficient global optimization (EGO) [7]. 

Encouraged by the success of EGO for single-objective optimization, efforts have been made to extend it to expensive multiobjective optimization [8]–[10]. A critical issue in multiobjective EGO (MOEGO) methods is how to generalize the EI function. Expected hypervolume improvement (EHVI) [9] is one of the most successful multiobjective EI metrics. For real-life applications, multiple expensive function evaluations, which rely on the computer or physical simulations, can often be performed simultaneously on multiple cores or machines. Consequently, much work has focused on developing parallel (or batch) MOEGO methods, where _q_ query points are generated in each iteration [6]. 

A natural extension of EHVI for parallel MOEGO is the multi-point EHVI (i.e., _q_ -EHVI) [11]. Using the hypervolume - indicator [12], _q_ EHVI can measure the potential improvement when adding _q_ query points simultaneously. However, it suffers from two major difficulties. Firstly, there is no analytical formula for the exact computation of _q_ -EHVI when _q ≥_ 2 [11]. Although Monte Carlo methods can be used, they could be computationally intensive when many samples are required for a low estimation error. Secondly, the dimension of the _q_ -EHVI maximization problem is _q × d_ where _d_ is the dimension of the original expensive multiobjective optimization problem (MOP). This makes maximization of _q_ -EHVI very difficult when the batch size _q_ increases. Alternatively, one can sequentially maximize the EHVI for _q_ times to obtain _q_ query points [11]. But this method could be very time-consuming when _q_ is large because the computation of EHVI is also complicated [13]. In this paper, we tackle these two problems by integrating hypervolume-based EI with decompositionbased methods. 

MOEA/D [14] is a basic evolutionary algorithmic framework for multiobjective optimization. It uses reference vectors (also called weight, direction, or preference vectors) to decompose a MOP in question into a number of scalarized functions and optimizes them in a collaborative manner. As shown in [10], [15], the MOEA/D framework can readily support parallel MOEGO and scales well with the batch size. Very recently, theoretical analyses in [16]–[18] suggest that the hypervolume of a set of objective vectors is equal to the mean of a scalarized function over a probability distribution of reference vectors. Therefore, widely used hypervolume-based multiobjective algorithms and decomposition-based methods are closely related to each other. 

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023 

2 

Under the MOEA/D framework, we develop a directionbased hypervolume improvement (denoted as _ID_ ) to ensure that the corresponding EI metric can be computed analytically. Specifically, our proposed improvement function _ID_ measures the amount of hypervolume improvement within an axis-parallel box induced by the modified Tchebycheff scalarization [19]. In this way, the expectation of _ID_ over the GP posterior, called expected direction-based hypervolume improvement (DirHV-EI), can be easily computed through a simple analytical formula, which is similar to the multiplied EI (mEI) [20]. Differences between DirHV-EI and mEI are discussed in the Supplementary File. The major contributions of our work are summarized as follows<sup>1</sup> . 

- We present DirHV-EI, a novel hypervolume-based EI that can be easily integrated into the MOEA/D framework. 

- We investigate the relationships between _ID_ and the other two representative improvement functions. Theoretical analysis indicates that the maximization of _ID_ can help to maximize both the weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement metrics. 

- We design a decomposition-based MOEGO algorithm, called DirHV-EGO, which uses a submodular-based greedy selection strategy to select _q_ query points from the candidate set. 

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section II introduces the related background. Section III details the DirHV-EI metric. Section IV presents the DirHV-EGO algorithm. Section V conducts numerical experiments to test the proposed method. Finally, section VI concludes the paper. 

### II. BACKGROUND 

- _A. Expensive Multiobjective Optimization_ 

A continuous MOP can be defined as 



where _F_ : Ω _�→_ R<sup>_m_</sup> consists _m_ objective functions, **_x_** is the decision variable, _fi_ ( **_x_** ) is the _i_ -th objective function, R<sup>_m_</sup> is the objective space, and Ω _⊆_ R<sup>_d_</sup> is the decision space. We assume all the objective functions are continuous, noise-free, but expensive to evaluate. 

Let **_u_** = ( _u_ 1 _, . . . , um_ )<sup>_T_</sup> _,_ **_v_** = ( _v_ 1 _, . . . , vm_ )<sup>_T_</sup> be two vectors in the objective space. **_u_** is said to dominate **_v_** , denotes as **_u_** _≺_ **_v_** , if and only if _ui ≤ vi_ for all _i_ = 1 _, . . . , m_ and _uj < vj_ for at least one index _j_ . **_u_** is said to weakly dominate **_v_** , denotes as **_u_** _⪯_ **_v_** , if and only if _ui ≤ vi_ for all _i_ = 1 _, . . . , m_ . **_u_** is said to strictly dominate **_v_** , denotes as **_u_** _<_ **_v_** , if and only if _ui < vi_ for all _i_ = 1 _, . . . , m_ . **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _∈_ Ω is Pareto optimal if there is no **_x_** _∈_ Ω such that _F_ ( **_x_** ) _≺ F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ). **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _∈_ Ω is weakly Pareto optimal if there does not exist another **_x_** _∈_ Ω such that _F_ ( **_x_** ) strictly dominates _F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ). The Pareto set (PS) is the set of all Pareto optimal points. The Pareto front (PF) is the image of PS in the objective space. 

### _B. Parallel Multiobjective Efficient Global Optimization_ 

Existing parallel MOEGO methods can be roughly classified into the following three categories according to their acquisition functions: 

_1) Parallel MOEGO Based on Single-objective EI:_ To directly use the single-objective EI, a simple and efficient strategy is to randomly transform the MOP into a scalarized function in each iteration and build a GP model to approximate the scalarized function. ParEGO [8] is representative of this category. Such approaches can be extended for parallel MOEGO by randomly creating _q_ scalarized functions in each iteration [21]. However, it could be challenging to directly build GP models for each scalarized function when the value of _q_ is large [22]. 

_2) Decomposition-based Parallel MOEGO:_ The methods in this category adopt the decomposition strategy [14] and build GP models for each objective but not the scalarized functions. Depending on the decomposition methods (e.g., scalarization functions) used, different multiobjective EI metrics such as expected Tchebycheff improvement (ETI) [10] and expected improvement of penalty-based boundary intersection (EPBII) [23] have been proposed and studied. A representative algorithm in this category is MOEA/D-EGO [10]. In each iteration, it maximizes the ETI values with respect to a number of subproblems and could generate a set of candidates. Then, _q_ query points can be easily selected from the candidates. However, there is no simple analytical expression for these EI metrics, and approximation methods have to be used for computing them [10], [23]. 

_3) Indicator-based Parallel MOEGO:_ Using the indicator metrics in multiobjective optimization, many multiobjective EI metrics have been proposed, such as hypervolume-based EI (i.e., EHVI) [9], Euclidean distance-based EI [24], and maximin distance-based EI [25]. Recently, some of them have been extended for parallel MOEGO, such as _q_ -EHVI [11]. In each iteration, the maximization of _q_ -EHVI could directly generate _q_ query points. However, it suffers from high computational overhead. Alternatively, Zhan et al. [26], [27] proposed an easy-to-compute metric to approximate the indicator-based EI and extended it to parallel optimization. Yang et al. [28] suggested dividing the bi-objective space into multiple subspaces, with multiple query points generated by maximizing the truncated EHVI [29] in each sub-space. Additionally, Gaudrie et al. [30] proposed the _q_ -mEI metric to rapidly reach a target point on the PF. 

Algorithm 1 outlines the general structure of parallel MOEGO methods that use the multiobjective EI (i.e., decomposition-based and indicator-based EI). In Line 1, some initial samples _{_ **_x_**<sup>_i_</sup> _}_<sup>_C_</sup> _i_ =1<sup>0are generated with a Design of Exper-</sup> iment (DOE) method, such as the Latin hypercube sampling. After that, a parallel MOEGO method follows a sequential decision-making framework. In each iteration, _q_ query points are selected and evaluated through four main steps: objective space normalization (if necessary), modeling, EI-based selection, and parallel evaluation of true objective vectors. These four components are repeated until the computational budget is exhausted.

Both hypervolume and scalarization function play an important role in multiobjective optimization. The following theorem implies the hypervolume metric defined in (2) can be expressed as the mean of a scalarized function over a probability distribution of reference vectors [16]–[18]. 

**Theorem 1** (Hypervolume scalarization [16]–[18]) **.** _The hypervolume of a set of objective vectors A with respect to a reference point_ **_r_** _is equivalent to the expectation of a scalarized function over a probability distribution of reference vectors:_

When _m_ = 2 or 3, EHVI can be exactly computed with time complexity of Θ( _nk_ log _nk_ ) [32], [33], where _nk_ is the number of nondominated points in _A_ . However, when _m >_ 3, existing methods for computing (7) are complicated and timeconsuming because it is NP-hard in _m_ [13], [34].

We note that there is no known analytical formula for the exact computation of _q_ -EHVI when _q ≥_ 2 [11], [35]. Moreover, _X_ is an arbitrary subset of Ω of size _q_ , rendering the _q_ -EHVI maximization complicated to solve when _q_ is large. An alternative is to use the sequential greedy approximation methods, such as the Kriging Believer (KB) [36]. As shown in Table I, such approximation conditions the EHVI on the previously selected query points and sequentially maximizes the EHVI for _q_ times to get _q_ query points [11].

### _B. Direction-based Hypervolume Improvement_ 

### III. DIRECTION-BASED HYPERVOLUME IMPROVEMENT AND ITS PROPERTIES 

In this section, we provide a new hypervolume-based EI based on the MOEA/D framework. We also present the connections of our proposed method with the weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement metrics. 

### _A. EHVI within an Axis-parallel Box_ 

The exact computation of EHVI is complicated when the nondominated region _U_ is irregular and high-dimensional. However, the values of EHVI within an axis-parallel box can be exactly and efficiently computed for problems with any number of objectives [34]. The following lemma provides a special case when the axis-parallel box is only bounded by an upper bound point. 

**Lemma 1.** _Let_ **_u_**<sup>_∗_</sup> = ( _u_<sup>_∗_</sup> 1<sup>_, . . . , u∗_</sup> _m_<sup>)</sup><sup>_be a point in the nondomi-_</sup> _nated region U , and IH_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_u_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) = � _U_<sup>_∗_1(</sup><sup>**_y_**</sup><sup>_≺_</sup><sup>**_u_**)</sup><sup>_d_</sup><sup>**_u_**</sup><sup>_bethehy-_</sup> _pervolume improvement of_ **_y_** _within U_<sup>_∗_</sup> = _{_ **_u_** _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> _|_ **_u_** _≺_ **_u_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _}. Let τi_ :=<sup>_u_</sup> _<u>i</u>_<sup>_∗_</sup> _σ_ ˆ<sup>_−_</sup> _i_<sup>_<u>µ</u>_</sup> (<sup>ˆ</sup> **_x_**<sup>_i_</sup><sup><u>(</u></sup> )<sup>**_x_**</sup><sup><u>)</u></sup> _. Then,_ 



_where_ Φ( _·_ ) _and ϕ_ ( _·_ ) _are the standard Gaussian cumulative distribution function (cdf) and probability density function (pdf), respectively._ 

Note that the RHS of (10) is similar to the product of singleobjective EIs, which can be easily computed. This property has been used for constructing effective approximations of EHVI [20], [26], [37]. Recently, it has also been extended for parallel MOEGO [27], [30]. However, these hypervolumebased EI metrics did not consider the connection between hypervolume and decomposition-based methods. To generate _q_ query points in each iteration, they either need to sequentially maximize a single-point EI for _q_ times [11], [27] or maximize a multi-point EI in R<sup>_m×q_</sup> [30]. It’s inefficient if the batch size _q_ is large. 

Recall that the parallel MOEGO methods based on the MOEA/D framework scale well as _q_ increases [10], [15]. It is natural to study whether it is feasible to integrate hypervolumebased EI with the MOEA/D framework. Clearly, Theorem 1, Lemma 1, and the geometric property of modified Tchebycheff scalarization [19] can inspire us to achieve this goal. 

As discussed earlier, there are two components in a multiobjective EI (4), the posterior distribution _p_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_x_** _, D_ ) and the improvement function _I_ ( **_y_** ). The posterior distribution is usually the same for different multiobjective EI metrics. Then, the key is how to define the improvement function. Here, we use the MOEA/D framework to define a new improvement function that is a special case of _IH_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_u_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) in Lemma 1. 

_1) Definition:_ As in Section II-C, suppose that _A_ is the set of all the evaluated objective vectors in _D_ . The attainment surface induced by _A_ , denoted as _AS_ , is defined as the boundary of the set _{_ **_v_** _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> _|∃_ **_a_** _∈ A_ : **_a_** _⪯_ **_v_** _}_ [38]. **_λ_** = ( _λ_ 1 _, . . . , λm_ )<sup>_T_</sup> is called a direction vector in this paper if _∥_ **_λ_** _∥_ 2 = 1 and _λi >_ 0 for all _i_ = 1 _, . . . , m_ . Let **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> = ( _z_ 1<sup>_∗, . . . , z_</sup> _m_<sup>_∗_)</sup><sup>_T_beaUtopianpoint,i.e.</sup><sup>_z_</sup> _i_<sup>_∗≤_min</sup><sup>**_x_**</sup><sup>_∈_Ω</sup><sup>_fi_(</sup><sup>**_x_**).</sup> Let **_ξ_** = ( _ξ_ 1 _, . . . , ξm_ )<sup>_T_</sup> be the intersection point of the attainment surface and the ray starting from **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> with direction **_λ_** . Then, we have [19], [39]: 



As illustrated in Fig. 2, the attainment surface can be regarded as an upper bound of the PF. In other words, the true PF must be below the attainment surface. Thus, **_ξ_** is an upper estimation of the intersection of the true PF and the ray starting from **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> with direction **_λ_** . 

**Definition 1** (Direction-based hypervolume improvement) **.** _For_ **_y_** = ( _y_ 1 _, . . . , ym_ )<sup>_T_</sup> _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> _, its direction-based hypervolume improvement (DirHVI) with respect to A and direction vector_ **_λ_** _is defined as_ 





_2) Basic Properties of ID:_ Fig. 2(a) illustrates the geometric meaning of _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) in the case of two objectives. With a given direction vector **_λ_** , _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) is the volume of the axisparallel box bounded by **_ξ_** and **_y_** when **_y_** _≺_ **_ξ_** . As shown in Fig. 2(b), we usually use multiple direction vectors at the same time. Let [ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _,_ **_r_** ] denote an axis-parallel box bounded by **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> and **_r_** , i.e., [ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _,_ **_r_** ] := _{_ **_u_** _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> _|_ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _⪯_ **_u_** _⪯_ **_r_** _}_ . _P_ **_λ_** is defined as a positive probability measure on the following set:

Considering all possible direction vectors sampled from _P_ **_λ_** , we would like to make the following comments: 

- When _m_ = 1, the computation of _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) essentially reduces to that of the standard single-objective improvement function. 

- If **_y_** _∈/ A_ is strictly dominated by **_r_** and is not dominated by any points in _A_ , there is at least one direction vector **_λ_** satisfying _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0 and vice versa. 

- _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) is monotonic with respect to Pareto dominance. Let **_y_**<sup>1</sup> _,_ **_y_**<sup>2</sup> _∈/ A_ be two different objective vectors that are strictly dominated by **_r_** and are not dominated by any points in _A_ . If **_y_**<sup>1</sup> _≺_ **_y_**<sup>2</sup> , we always have _ID_ ( **_y_**<sup>1</sup> _|_ **_λ_** ) _≥ ID_ ( **_y_**<sup>2</sup> _|_ **_λ_** ) for any direction vector, and there exists at least one direction vector **_λ_**<sup>_∗_</sup> such that _ID_ ( **_y_**<sup>1</sup> _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) _> ID_ ( **_y_**<sup>2</sup> _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ), e.g., **_λ_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _∈_ arg max **_λ_** _ID_ ( **_y_**<sup>2</sup> _|_ **_λ_** ).

### _3) Maximization of ID and Pareto Optimality:_ 

**Proposition 1.** _For a direction vector_ **_λ_** _. If_ **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _is the optimal solution to_ 



_and ID_ ( _F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0 _. Then_ **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _is Pareto optimal._ 

**Proposition 2.** _Suppose_ **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _∈_ Ω _is Pareto optimal to_ (1) _, F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) _∈/ A. Then there exists at least one direction vector_ **_λ_** _such that ID_ ( _F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0 _._ 

If the attainment surface induced by _A_ does not include the whole PF, these two properties imply that we can always find a direction **_λ_** and improve _A_ by maximizing (12). Therefore, our proposed improvement function _ID_ can be used as a scalarization function to deal with nonconvex PFs, whereas optimization of the weighted sum scalarization may not lead to any improvement when the PF is not convex [40, Sec. 4.7.4]. 

### _C. Relationship with Weighted Hypervolume Improvement_ 

Let _γ_ : _U →_ R<sup>+</sup> be a positive weight function. Given **_y_** _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> , the hypervolume improvement (5) can be extended as a weighted hypervolume improvement [41], [42]: 



Theorem 1 suggests that the hypervolume is closely related to a scalarized function. Here, Proposition 3 tells us the average value of _ID_ with respect to direction vectors is equal to the weighted hypervolume improvement with a specific weight function _γ_ ( **_u_** ). 

**Definition 2** (Average direction-based hypervolume improvement) **.** _The average direction-based hypervolume improvement (ADirHVI) of_ **_y_** _with respect to A is defined as_ 



**Proposition 3.** _The average direction-based hypervolume improvement is equal to a weighted hypervolume improvement:_ 



_where the weight function is the probability that vector_ **_u_** _dominates the vector_ **_ξ_** _induced by_ **_λ_** _, i.e., γ_ ( **_u_** ) = P( **_u_** _≺_ **_ξ_** ( **_λ_** )) _._ 

As shown in Fig. 3, _IAD_ ( **_y_** ) is different from the standard hypervolume improvement _IH_ ( **_y_** ). Due to the weight function P( **_u_** _≺_ **_ξ_** ( **_λ_** )), _IAD_ prefers the vectors that could dominate more intersection points **_ξ_** induced by **_λ_** _∼ P_ **_λ_** . Proposition 3 implies that if we maximize _IAD_ ( **_y_** ) in (15), then the weighted hypervolume improvement defined in (16) also achieves its maximum. From Definition 2, for a direction vector **_λ_** sampled from _P_ **_λ_** , _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) can be regarded as an unbiased estimation of _IAD_ ( **_y_** ). It implies that if an algorithm maximizes _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) for a randomly selected **_λ_** at each iteration, its behavior may be similar to those algorithms for maximizing the weighted hypervolume improvement _IAD_ ( **_y_** ). 

For multiobjective optimization, there are usually an infinite number of Pareto optimal solutions. Thus, it is impossible to use a single solution **_y_**<sup>_∗_</sup> to maximize the (weighted) hypervolume metric _H_ ( _·,_ **_r_** ) (see Fig. 3). To obtain multiple promising solutions, we can sample _N_ direction vectors, denoted as **_λ_**<sup>1</sup> _, . . . ,_ **_λ_**<sup>_N_</sup> , that are uniformly distributed in the objective space (see Fig. 2). Then, _N_ subproblems can be defined and each one concentrates on locating a promising solution in the subregion _U_ **_λ_** _i_ :

This proposition indicates that with the same uncertain level, EID is consistent with Pareto dominance; and if two different points have the same expected objective values, EID favors one with higher uncertainty. It is desirable that an expected improvement function have this monotonicity property [26]. 

### IV. PROPOSED ALGORITHM USING EID 

### _A. Algorithm_ 

Our proposed algorithm, named DirHV-EGO, adopts a similar framework as Algorithm 1 except for Line 5, where a two-step method is used to select _q_ query points. It works as follows: 



2) Select _q_ query points from _Q_ (Algorithm 3). 

In step 1, we use _N_ direction vectors to define _N_ subproblems, which are extensions of (17) for expensive optimization: 



Then, we use the MOEA/D framework to solve these subproblems collaboratively, rather than solving them independently by single-objective algorithms. It is because these subproblems are related to one another. When two direction vectors are close, the optimal solutions of their subproblems should be similar. This similarity among subproblems allows us to leverage the neighborhood structure in MOEA/D and solve (24) in a single run. In contrast, independently solving all the subproblems in (24) ignores the similarity among subproblems and is less efficient. 

In step 2, we propose a submodular-based greedy selection strategy to select _q_ query points from the candidate set _Q_ , which is a finite set. It is noted that directly maximizing the multi-point EHVI requires finding _q_ optimal points in the highdimensional decision space, i.e., R<sup>_q×d_</sup> . In contrast, selecting _q_ points from the finite set _Q_ should be easier. 

For many real-world problems, each objective can have a different range, and objective space normalization is often required. In this article, a simple normalization method [26] is used in Line 3 of Algorithm 1. For each _i ∈{_ 1 _, . . . , m}_ , the _i_ -th normalized objective value of any point **_x_** _∈D_ is obtained through ( _fi_ ( **_x_** ) _− zi_<sup>_min_</sup> ) _/_ ( _zi_<sup>_max_</sup> _− zi_<sup>_min_</sup> ), where _zi_<sup>_min_</sup> = min<sup>and</sup><sup>_z_</sup> _i_<sup>_max_</sup> = max **_x_** _∈D_<sup>_fi_(</sup><sup>**_x_**)</sup> **_x_** _∈D_<sup>_fi_(</sup><sup>**_x_**).</sup> 

### _B. Using MOEA/D to maximize_ EID 

MOEA/D [14] is a basic algorithmic framework in multiobjective evolutionary computation. Over the past few decades, many multiobjective algorithms have been developed using the MOEA/D framework [43], [44]. Some of them can be easily extended to optimize (24) in a single run. In this section, we take MOEA/D-GR [45] as an example to maximize the EID criterion for finding the candidate set _Q_ . The main steps are summarized in Algorithm 2. 

Our approach requires _N_ direction vectors **_λ_**<sup>1</sup> _, . . . ,_ **_λ_**<sup>_N_</sup> sampled from _P_ **_λ_** . In Line 1, we firstly generate _N_ weight vectors **_w_**<sup>1</sup> _, . . . ,_ **_w_**<sup>_N_</sup> using the simplex-lattice design [46] or incremental lattice design [47]. Then, for each weight vector **_w_** = ( _w_ 1 _, . . . , wm_ )<sup>_T_</sup> , we can construct a direction vector **_λ_** = ( _λ_ 1 _, . . . , λm_ )<sup>_T_</sup> through

For each current solution ˜ ˜ ˜ ˆ **_x_**<sup>_k_</sup> in the _Tr_ -neighborhood of **_x_**<sup>_j_</sup> , replace **_x_**<sup>_k_</sup> by **_x_** if EID(ˆ **_x_** _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_k_</sup> ) _>_ EID(˜ **_x_**<sup>_k_</sup> _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_k_</sup> ).

- 11: If the stopping conditions are satisfied, then discard the duplicate individuals and output the final population; otherwise, go to **Step 2** . 

In Line 2, _N_ individuals _{_ **_x_** ˜<sup>1</sup> _, . . . ,_ ˜ **_x_**<sup>_N_</sup> _}_ are randomly gen˜ erated. For _i_ = 1 _, . . . , N_ , **_x_**<sup>_i_</sup> is regarded as the current solution for the _i_ -th subproblem. The _Tm_ -neighborhood and ˜ _Tr_ -neighborhood of **_x_**<sup>_i_</sup> are the set of _Tm_ closest and _Tr_ closest subproblems to the _i_ -th subproblem in terms of the Euclidean distances among direction vectors, respectively [45]. 

In Line 9, the global replacement scheme proposed in MOEA/D-GR is used, which can avoid the mismatch between solutions and subproblems. For each subproblem at each generation, suppose **_x_** ˆ _∈_ Ω is a newly generated solution through the crossover, mutation, and repair operators. We use EID to select the most appropriate subproblem _j_ for **_x_** ˆ: 



For all the index _k_ in the _Tr_ -neighborhood of subproblem _j_ , if EID(ˆ **_x_** _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_k_</sup> ) _>_ EID(˜ **_x_**<sup>_k_</sup> _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_k_</sup> ), we replace **_x_** ˜<sup>_k_</sup> with **_x_** ˆ. Using this strategy, one high-quality solution could update multiple subproblems in Line 9 and accelerate the convergence of the algorithm. Meanwhile, each **_x_** ˆ cannot update too many subproblems because _Tr_ is often much smaller than _N_ . It helps maintain the diversity of the population. 

### _C. Submodularity-based Batch Selection_ 

MOEA/D-EGO adopts an ad-hoc method to select a few solutions for true objective function evaluations from a set of candidates. We propose to formulate the selection task as a submodular maximization problem. 

As in Section II-C, let _X_ be the set of _q_ points. We can generalize _ID_ and _IAD_ to measure the improvement when adding _F_ ( _X_ ) to _A_ as follows: 





Clearly, _IAD_ ( _F_ ( _X_ )) is a Pareto compliant indicator [41]. However, it is very difficult, if not impossible, to have an analytical and efficient way for computing the expectation of _ID_ ( _F_ ( _X_ ) _|_ **_λ_** ) where _F_ ( _X_ ) are random. 

To facilitate the computation in the batch selection, instead of E[ _ID_ ( _F_ ( _X_ ) _|_ **_λ_** )], we use its lower bound max **_x_** _∈X_ E[ _ID_ ( _F_ ( **_x_** ) _|_ **_λ_** )] to measure the improvement of adding _X_ to _D_ along the direction **_λ_** . Considering the randomness of _fi_ ( **_x_** ) and multiple direction vectors, we measure the contribution of _X_ to _D_ in DirHV-EGO by: 



Then, the batch selection can be modeled as the following submodular maximization problem: 



The above set function _h_ ( _X_ ) is a monotone submodular function since it’s the same as the set function of the facility location problem in [48]. A submodular maximization problem is NP-hard in general. The greedy algorithm is commonly used. It provides a (1 _−_ 1 _/e_ )-approximation guarantee when the submodular function is monotone [49]. In Algorithm 3, we present an implementation of the greedy algorithm for (30). The algorithm initializes _X_ = _∅_ . At each iteration, it adds to _X_ the following element:

- _q_ : the batch size. 

**Output:** _X_ : the set of _q_ selected solutions.

### V. EXPERIMENTAL STUDIES 

In this section, we make the following comparisons to empirically study the performance of our proposed method. For a fair comparison, all the compared methods use the same framework shown in Algorithm 1, except for Line 5. 

- 1) To show the effectiveness of DirHV-EI, we first set _q_ = 1 and compare our proposed method against two representative multiobjective EI criteria, namely, EHVI<sup>2</sup> [9], and ETI<sup>3</sup> [10]. 

- 2) The major motivation for proposing EID is to facilitate hypervolume-based parallel MOEGO. Three related parallel MOEGOs, including KB&EHVI-EGO [11], PEIMEGO [27], and MOEA/D-EGO [10], are then selected for comparison under different settings of batch size _q_ . 

### _A. Experiment Settings_ 

_1) Test Instances:_ In our experimental studies, three test suites are used, including the ZDT test suite [50], DTLZ test suite [51], and five real-world MOPs [52]. Settings of the number of objectives and the number of decision variables for each test problem are shown in Table II.

|---|---|---|
|ZDT test suite [50]|2|8|
|DTLZ test suite [51]|3|6|
|RE1 (Hatch cover design [53])|2|2|
|RE2 (Four bar truss design [54])|2|4|
|RE3 (Two bar truss design [55])|3|3|
|RE4 (Welded beam design [56])|3|4|
|RE5 (Rocket injector design [57])|3|4|



_2) Performance Metrics:_ In our study, the inverted generational distance plus (IGD+) [58] and the hypervolume [12] metrics are used to measure the algorithm performance. 

_Inverted generational distance plus (IGD+):_ Let _P_ be the obtained approximate PF and _P_<sup>_∗_</sup> be a set of well-distributed objective vectors sampled from PF. The IGD+ is defined as 



where _|P_<sup>_∗_</sup> _|_ is the number of points in _P_<sup>_∗_</sup> . A smaller IGD+ value indicates the algorithm performs better. 

> 2The codes of exact calculation algorithms for EHVI with _m_ = 2 and 3 are kindly provided by the authors of [32], [33]. 

> 3The approximation method proposed in [10] is used to estimate the values of ETI. We note that the RHS of Eq. (17) in [10] should be: ( _µ_<sup>2</sup> 1<sup>+</sup><sup>_σ_</sup> 1<sup>2)Φ(</sup><sup>_α_)+</sup> ( _µ_<sup>2</sup> 2<sup>+</sup><sup>_σ_</sup> 2<sup>2)Φ(</sup><sup>_−α_) + (</sup><sup>_µ_1 +</sup><sup>_µ_2)</sup><sup>_τφ_(</sup><sup>_α_).(SeeAppendixBof[10])</sup> 

_Hypervolume:_ The IGD+ metric requires a set of reference vectors _P_<sup>_∗_</sup> , which is unavailable when the true PF is unknown. Therefore, the hypervolume metric is used for performance assessment when dealing with real-world problems. As recommended in [52], we calculate _H_ ( _P,_ **_r_** ) in the normalized objective space [0 _,_ 1]<sup>_m_</sup> and the reference point **_r_** is set as (1 _._ 1 _, . . . ,_ 1 _._ 1)<sup>_T_</sup> . A larger hypervolume value means that the set _P_ is a better approximation for the PF in terms of both convergence and diversity. 

- _3) General settings:_ 

- Number of initial samples _C_ 0: 11 _d −_ 1. 

- Method to generate the initial samples: optimal Latin hypercube sampling [59]. 

- Maximal number of function evaluations _Ctotal_ : 200 for _m_ = 2 and 300 for _m_ = 3. 

- Number of independent runs: 21. 

_4) Parameter settings of GP model:_ To have a fair comparison, the DACE toolbox [60] is used to build the GP model for each algorithm. The related parameters are set as follows. 

- The regression function: _regpoly0_ . 

- The correlation function _c_ ( **_x_** _,_ **_x_**<sup>_′_</sup> ): _corrgauss_ . 

- Range of each hyperparameter _θi_ : [10<sup>_−_6</sup> _,_ 10<sup>2</sup> ]. 

_5) Inner optimizer:_ The differential evolution (DE) algorithm and MOEA/D-GR are used as the inner optimizers to maximize the EI functions. Detailed settings are as follows. 

- Population size: The population size _N_ is set as 200 for _m_ = 2 and 210 for _m_ = 3, and the initial population is randomly generated. 

- Termination condition: The maximum number of generations is set as 50. 

- Reproduction operators: The DE operators ( _F_ = 0 _._ 5, _CR_ = 1) and polynomial mutation (Index = 20, _pm_ = 1 _/d_ ) are used for offspring generation [61]. 

- Other control parameters for MOEA/D-GR: As in [45], we set _Tm_ = _Tr_ = 0 _._ 1 _× N_ and _δ_ = 0 _._ 8. 

All the algorithms are coded based on the MATLAB platform PlatEMO [62]. To analyze the experimental results, the Wilcoxon rank-sum test at a significance level of 0.05 is used to conduct a significant difference analysis. As a result of the Wilcoxon rank-sum test, the symbols +, _−_ , and _≈_ indicate the value obtained by a method is significantly better than, worse than, and competitive with that obtained by DirHV-EI based method, respectively. 

### _B. Effectiveness of DirHV-EI_ 

In this section, we set _q_ = 1 and conducted a set of numerical experiments to study the performance of four EI metrics, including EHVI, ADirHV-EI, ETI, and DirHV-EI. ADirHV-EI is defined as the expectation of _IAD_ ( **_y_** ) over the posterior _p_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_x_** _, D_ ). By Proposition 3, ADirHV-EI is a weighted EHVI with _γ_ ( **_u_** ) = P( **_u_** _≺_ **_ξ_** ( **_λ_** )), whereas EHVI can be regarded as a special weighted EHVI with _γ_ ( **_u_** ) = 1. Given _N_ direction vectors sampled from _P_ **_λ_** , Definition 2 tells us ADirHV-EI can be approximated by DirHV-EI:

### _C. Experimental Results on ZDT and DTLZ_ 

We first compare ADirHV-EI with EHVI. Both of them are maximized by the DE algorithm in each iteration. Table III presents the statistics of the IGD+ values obtained by different EI metrics. From the results, we can observe that ADirHVEI, i.e. the mean of DirHV-EI with respect to _P_ **_λ_** , performs similarly to EHVI on 7 out of 12 test problems. On ZDT13, DTLZ2, and DTLZ6, ADirHV-EI performs slightly better than EHVI. Therefore, the empirical results align with our statements in Proposition 3. Besides, it indicates that when the computational budget is limited, the performance of EHVI can be improved with a carefully designed weight function _γ_ ( **_u_** ). 

Then, we compare our proposed DirHV-EI, i.e. E[ _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** )], and ETI, i.e. E[ _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** )], under the MOEA/D framework. In each iteration, we sample _N_ direction vectors to define _N_ subproblems as in (24). To make the comparison fair, the subproblems defined by both DirHV-EI and ETI are maximized through MOEA/D-GR, and the individual with the highest EI values in the final population is selected as the query point. Other settings are the same. 

Table III shows that DirHV-EI can achieve mean IGD+ values that are comparable to or even lower than those attained by ETI. Besides, Fig. 4 shows that DirHV-EI converges faster than ETI on ZDT1 and ZDT2. A major reason is that the values of _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) are directly related to **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> (see Fig. 1 in the Supplementary File). When **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is inaccurately estimated, the ETI based algorithm may converge slowly. By contrast, the values of _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) mainly rely on the intersection points **_ξ_** and may be less affected by **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> . 

Table III also indicates that DirHV-EI using the MOEA/D framework outperforms ADirHV-EI using the DE algorithm on many test problems. This suggests that a multiobjective algorithm may be more effective for optimizing the multiobjective EI than a single-objective algorithm. 

This section compares DirHV-EGO with the following three parallel MOEGO algorithms, i.e., KB&EHVI-EGO [11], PEIM-EGO [27], and MOEA/D-EGO [10]. The corresponding multiobjective EI criteria are listed as follows. 

- Kriging Believer and EHVI (KB&EHVI): KB&EHVI is a sequential greedy approximation to _q_ -EHVI. As shown in Table I, it conditions the EHVI on the previously selected query points and sequentially maximizes the EHVI for _q_ times to get _q_ query points. 

- Pseudo Expected Improvement Matrix (PEIM): The PEIM metric is defined as the product of an influence function and the expected improvement matrix [26]. By sequentially maximizing the PEIM metric for _q_ times in each iteration, one can get the _q_ query points without updating the GP models. There are three variants of PEIM. Here, the hypervolume-based PEIM is used. 

- Expected Tchebycheff improvement (ETI): ETI is also designed under the MOEA/D framework. Since it may converge slowly with a fixed setting of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> , we adaptively update **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> when maximizing ETI [63]. Besides, the FuzzyCM used in the original MOEA/D-EGO is removed to improve its performance [21]. 

Table IV presents the mean IGD+ values of the approximation Pareto optimal solutions obtained by the four algorithms. From the results, we can find that DirHV-EGO obtains the best mean IGD+ values on ZDT1-2 and DTLZ1-2 with different settings of batch size. On ZDT3, DTLZ3, and DTLZ5, DirHVEGO obtains better or similar mean IGD+ values compared with others with different settings of _q_ . Fig. 5 shows the convergence profiles of different algorithms in terms of the log IGD+ values on all the test problems when _q_ = 5. We can find that DirHV-EGO significantly outperforms the others on ZDT1-3, DTLZ1-3, and DTLZ5.

The mean IGD+ values obtained by all the algorithms on ZDT4 are greater than 30. It indicates that ZDT4 is difficult to solve with a limited budget [10]. When _q_ = 1, Table III shows that the decomposition-based methods could obtain lower mean IGD+ values than EHVI-EGO on ZDT4. However, Table IV shows that KB&EHVI-EGO and PEIM-EGO outperform DirHV-EGO and MOEA/D-EGO when _q ≥_ 5. It is because the _f_ 2 of ZDT4 is difficult to optimize and should be minimized first with more budget. But DirHV-EGO and MOEA/D-EGO waste computational resources for maintaining the diversity of query points when the batch size is large. 

On ZDT6 and DTLZ4, KB&EHVI-EGO gets the best mean IGD+ values, whereas DirHV-EGO performs similarly on most test instances except on ZDT6 with _q_ = 10. On DTLZ6, MOEA/D-EGO performs the best in terms of the IGD+ metric, followed by DirHV-EGO. But Table III shows that DirHV-EI could get lower mean IGD+ values than ETI on DTLZ6 when we use a fixed setting of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> . It indicates the adaptive adjustment strategy of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> we used for MOEA/D-EGO does improve its performance. On DTLZ7, MOEA/D-EGO outperforms the others when _q_ = 2 and performs similarly with KB&EHVI-EGO and DirHV-EGO when _q ≥_ 5. 

In summary, we can conclude that our proposed method is competitive compared with the three parallel MOEGO methods with different settings of batch size. 

### _D. Discussion on Running Time_ 

_1) Analysis of the Computational Complexity:_ In each iteration of DirHV-EGO, the computational overhead mainly 

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023 

11 

consists of three parts: training the GP models, maximizing the EI metrics, and selecting _q_ query points from candidates. For training the GP models for all the objectives, the computational complexity is _O_ ( _mn_<sup>3</sup> ) with _n_ denoting the number of observations. When maximizing the DirHV-EI under the MOEA/D framework, the computational overhead mainly includes the time for calculating _µ_ ˆ _i_ ( **_x_** ), _σ_ ˆ _i_ ( **_x_** ), and EID( **_x_** _|_ **_λ_** ). Once the GP models have been constructed, each prediction requires _O_ ( _n_ ) for _µ_ ˆ _i_ ( **_x_** ) and _O_ ( _n_<sup>2</sup> ) for _σ_ ˆ _i_ ( **_x_** ) [9]. As for EID( **_x_** _|_ **_λ_** ), Proposition 5 provides a simple formula with a complexity of _O_ ( _m_ ). Then, it requires _O_ ( _mN_ ) to find the most appropriate subproblem, i.e., (26), in each iteration of Algorithm 2. Therefore, the computational complexity for maximizing the EI metrics is _O_ (( _mn_<sup>2</sup> + _mN_ ) _Ntmax_ ). When selecting _q_ points from _N_ candidates, it requires _O_ ( _mN_<sup>2</sup> ) to compute the values of EID( **_x_** _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_j_</sup> ) for all **_x_** _∈ Q_ and 1 _≤ j ≤ N_ . Then, it requires _O_ ( _N_ ) in each iteration of Algorithm 3. Therefore, selecting _q_ query points from candidates runs in _O_ ( _mN_<sup>2</sup> + _qN_ ). Assuming _q < N_ , the complexity in each iteration of DirHV-EGO is _O_ ( _mn_<sup>3</sup> +( _mn_<sup>2</sup> + _mN_ ) _Ntmax_ ). Intuitively, the complexity of DirHV-EGO is cubic to parameter _n_ , quadratic to parameter _N_ , and linear to parameters _m_ and _tmax_ . 

_2) Comparison of Computational Time:_ This section empirically studies the running time of DirHV-EGO and three other parallel MOEGO algorithms with respect to different settings of batch size. The number of EGO iterations is set as 50 for different algorithms. Assume the true function evaluations of _q_ query points obtained in each iteration are performed in parallel, e.g., in _q_ machines. Then, we mainly focus on the computational times of the algorithm itself. All the experiments are conducted on the same hardware platform (CPU: i7-9700, Memory: 64GB). The experimental results on DTLZ2 are shown in Fig. 6. The results show that the running times for both KB&EHVI-EGO and PEIM-EGO increase rapidly with increasing _q_ . In contrast, the CPU times for DirHV-EGO and MOEA/D-EGO increase much more slowly. It demonstrates the computational advantage of decomposition-based methods for parallel expensive multiobjective optimization. 

When _m_ = 3, the time complexity in each iteration of KB&EHVI-EGO is _O_ ( _qmn_<sup>3</sup> + ( _mn_<sup>2</sup> + _nk_ log _nk_ ) _qNtmax_ ) with _nk_ denoting the number of nondominated points. When _q_ = 1, _mN_ is much larger than _nk_ log _nk_ in our experimental settings. Then, KB&EHVI-EGO could be more efficient than DirHV-EGO. It is consistent with the experimental results shown in Fig. 6. However, when _q >_ 3, DirHV-EGO outperforms KB&EHVI-EGO in terms of CPU time. When _q_ = 15, the mean computational time is 9066 seconds (about 2.5 hours) for KB&EHVI-EGO, but only 818 seconds (about 13.6 minutes) for DirHV-EGO. In this case, DirHV-EGO is 11 times faster than KB&EHVI-EGO. PEIM-EGO uses a similar framework to KB&EHVI-EGO. When _m_ = 3, they perform similarly in terms of CPU time. 

From Fig. 6, we can also find that DirHV-EGO is more efficient than MOEA/D-EGO. There are two reasons: i) the ETI approximation is more complicated than the computation of DirHV-EI, and ii) MOEA/D-EGO needs to adjust **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> adaptively at each iteration, whereas DirHV-EGO can work well on most test problems with a simple setting of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> .

### _E. Experimental Results on Real-world Problems_ 

In this section, DirHV-EGO is further tested on five realworld MOPs. The batch size is set as 5, and other settings are the same as in Section V-A. The statistics of hypervolume values are summarized in Table V. It can be seen that DirHVEGO significantly outperforms the other algorithms on RE1-4. On RE5, EHVI-EGO performs the best, followed by DirHVEGO. Fig. 7 plots the final solutions obtained in the run with the median hypervolume value of each algorithm on RE5. With a limited computational budget, DirHV-EGO does not approximate the upper right corner of PF very well compared with KB&EHVI-EGO. But it could find more nondominated solutions in the upper left corner of PF.

### VI. CONCLUSION 

This paper has introduced expected direction-based hypervolume improvement (DirHV-EI) that integrates hypervolumebased EI with the MOEA/D framework. The proposed DirHVEI can be analytically computed with the complexity of _O_ ( _m_ ). Theoretical analysis indicates that maximization of our proposed metric could also serve to maximize both the weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement metrics. To solve expensive MOPs, we have designed an algorithm called DirHV-EGO. At each iteration, it maximizes the values of DirHV-EI with respect to a number of direction vectors simultaneously. Then, a submodularity-based batch selection strategy is proposed to select multiple query points for parallel computing. 

Empirical results demonstrate that DirHV-EGO is an efficient and effective method for parallel expensive multiobjective optimization. However, there are still some limitations. 

- DirHV-EGO requires more direction vectors as the number of objectives increases in order to better explore the search space. But its computational complexity is quadratic to the number of direction vectors, which indicates DirHV-EGO may be inefficient when there are too many objectives. 

- DirHV-EGO uses _N_ uniformly distributed direction vectors, which may not be the best setting for MOPs with irregular PF. 

- A continuous MOP usually has an infinite number of Pareto optimal solutions. But DirHV-EGO can only find a limited number of optimal solutions to approximate the set of all the optimal solutions. 

- DirHV-EGO uses GP models to approximate the objectives. Due to the fact that GP models are prone to suffer from the curse of dimensionality, it can be a challenge for DirHV-EGO to deal with high-dimensional MOPs. 

Apart from applying DirHV-EGO to real-life problems, there are several interesting research topics for future work: 

- Extensions to other complex multiobjective scenarios [6], such as constrained, multi-fidelity, and noisy. 

- Integration of DirHV-EGO with reference vector adjustment methods [44]. 

- DirHV-EGO with Pareto set learning [64], which has been shown to be able to identify a low-dimensional manifold to approximate the entire PS. 

- Integration of DirHV-EI with other surrogate models, such as probabilistic classifier [65].

He is a Chair Professor at the Department of Computer Science, City University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong. His main research interests include evolutionary computation, optimization, machine learning, and their applications. Prof. Zhang has been a highly cited 



researcher in computer science since 2016. He is an Associate Editor of the IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION and IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON CYBERNETICS.

# Supplementary Material: Hypervolume-Guided Decomposition for Parallel Expensive Multiobjective Optimization 

Liang Zhao and Qingfu Zhang, _Fellow, IEEE_ 

**_Abstract_ —This document is the supplementary material to the article entitled “Hypervolume-Guided Decomposition for Parallel Expensive Multiobjective Optimization”. This document includes six sections. In Section I, we present preliminary techniques used in this study. Section II provides detailed proofs of Lemma 1 and Propositions 3-6. Section III offers additional empirical analysis of the performance of DirHV-EGO. In Section IV, we present technical implementation details of MOEA/D-EGO. Section V delves into a comparison between DirHV-EI and mEI, highlighting their respective differences. Finally, Section VI includes additional experimental results.** 

### I. PRELIMINARY TECHNIQUES

It is easy to verify that _g_<sup>_mte_</sup> ( **_ξ_**<sup>_′_</sup> _|_ **_λ_** _,_ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) = _g_<sup>_mte_</sup> ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** _,_ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) and **_ξ_**<sup>_′_</sup> lies on the ray starting from **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> with direction **_λ_** . 

Using (14), the intersection point **_ξ_** induced by the direction vector **_λ_** is 







As illustrated in Fig. 1, (19) implies that _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) measures the distance between **_ξ_** and **_ξ_**<sup>_′_</sup> when **_y_** _≺_ **_ξ_** . **_ξ_**<sup>_′_</sup> is the intersection point of the attainment surface and the ray starting from **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> with direction **_λ_** when adding **_y_** to _A_ . Consequently, the values of _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) are highly impacted by **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> . Thus, the determination of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is essential for the maximization of _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ). When **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is inaccurately estimated, the ETI based algorithm may converge slowly. By contrast, the values of _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) mainly rely on the intersection points **_ξ_** and may be less affected by **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> . 

The geometric property of _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) also indicates that _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0 if and only if _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0. Therefore, the probability of improvement (PI) criteria [10] induced by _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) and _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) are equivalent, i.e., P( _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0) = P( _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0) when **_y_** are random. However, their expectations, i.e. E[ _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** )] and E[ _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** )], are not equivalent since the values of _IT_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) and _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) are different for the same **_y_** and **_λ_** .

_3) Comments on the Weighted Hypervolume Improvement:_ The hypervolume indicator is widely used in multiobjective optimization. A major reason is that it is compliant with the Pareto dominance [15]. The weighted (or generalized) hypervolume indicator [16] is a variant of the standard hypervolume indicator. When the weight function _γ_ : R<sup>_m_</sup> _→_ R<sup>+</sup> is a positive function, the weighted hypervolume indicator is also compliant with the Pareto dominance [16], [17]. This indicator can also be used to define a weighted hypervolume improvement for expensive multiobjective optimization [18]. To ensure that the weight function remains positive, researchers typically resort to defining the weight function through probability density functions (pdfs) [17]–[19]. But the resulting weighted hypervolume metrics are usually more difficult to compute than the standard hypervolume indicator [15]. 

Fig. 4. Illustrations of the maximization of _ID_ and _IT_ with the same direction vector **_λ_** . For any vector in the blue dashed line, we have _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) = _ID_ ( _F_ ( **_x_**<sup>_∗_</sup> ) _|_ **_λ_** ). 

Proposition 3 tells us _IAD_ is also a weighted hypervolume improvement metric with _γ_ ( **_u_** ) = P( **_u_** _≺_ **_ξ_** ( **_λ_** )). The weight function _γ_ ( **_u_** ) = P( **_u_** _≺_ **_ξ_** ( **_λ_** )) is strictly positive for any **_u_** in the interior of _U_ . Therefore, it also has desirable properties like other (weighted) hypervolume improvement metrics. For instance, _IAD_ is compliant with the Pareto dominance for the objective vectors in the nondominated region _U_ .

### III. FURTHER ANALYSIS OF DIRHV-EGO 

This section is divided into four parts. 

- 1) Section III-A empirically analyzes the sensitivity of DirHV-EGO to the Utopian point **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> and batch size _q_ . 

- 2) Section III-B discusses the effectiveness of the major components in DirHV-EGO. 

- 3) Section III-C explores the performance of DirHV-EGO on many-objective optimization problems (MaOPs). 

- 4) Section III-D contains more discussions on the performance of DirHV-EGO.

_1) Sensitivity to the Utopian Point_ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> _:_ To investigate the impacts of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> on the performance of DirHV-EGO, some representative values (e.g., _−_ 1 _, −_ 0 _._ 1 _, −_ 0 _._ 01 _, −_ 0 _._ 0001 _, −_ 0 _._ 000001 and 0) of _zi_<sup>_∗_areemployedtotestontheZDTandDTLZ</sup> test instances. The experimental results are shown in Fig. 5. Intuitively, DirHV-EGO is not very sensitive to **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> on many

_2) Sensitivity to the Number of Batch Size q:_ When the total budget _Ctotal_ is fixed, a single-objective parallel EGO algorithm is reported to get a poorer performance as _q_ increases for different problems [20]. However, it is not always true for expensive multiobjective optimization. Fig. 6 shows that the performance of DirHV-EGO on several test problems, such as ZDT3 and DTLZ6, does get worse when _q_ increases, likely due to the decrease in the number of iterations required to update the GP models, resulting in poorer approximations of the objectives. 

However, on DTLZ2 and DTLZ5, DirHV-EGO could perform slightly better with a larger _q_ . On ZDT1 and ZDT6, DirHV-EGO performs best when the batch size is five. On DTLZ3, DirHV-EGO obtains the lowest mean log IGD+ value when _q_ = 8. It indicates that adding multiple query points in each iteration can be better than sequentially adding a number of samples (i.e., _q_ = 1) for some expensive MOPs. This is because a multiobjective optimization algorithm should take both convergence and diversity into consideration. It could be helpful if the estimated PF can be improved from different regions by adding a diversity of samples simultaneously. 

In summary, there is no best choice of batch size for different expensive black-box MOPs, and the commonly-used setting, i.e., _q_ = 5, is acceptable for most problems. 

### _B. Effectiveness of Major Components in DirHV-EGO_ 

_1) Effectiveness of the Proposed Batch Selection Strategy:_ To study the effectiveness of the proposed submodularitybased batch selection strategy, we develop two variants of DirHV-EGO, denoted as DirHV-EGO-R and DirHV-EGO-K. These two variants differ from DirHV-EGO only in how to select multiple query points from the candidate set _Q_ (i.e., Algorithm 3). Specifically, DirHV-EGO-R randomly selects _q_ query points in each iteration, whereas DirHV-EGO-K uses the K-means-based selection method [7]. We note that the candidates whose EID values are zeros should be removed from _Q_ before using the random selection or the K-means algorithm. Otherwise, low-quality candidates may be selected. The comparison results are shown in Table I. 

From the results on ZDT4, DTLZ1, DTLZ3, and DTLZ4, we can find that DirHV-EGO performs similarly to DirHVEGO-R. Probably, the quality of each point in _Q_ is similar when the test problems are difficult to solve. For the other test problems, both DirHV-EGO-K and DirHV-EGO outperform the variant using random selection. This is because some subproblems might be unnecessary [21], and a proper batch selection method is usually required. 

The K-means-based method uses the distances between the decision variables and EI values of candidates to balance the

diversity and convergence of the selected query points. In contrast, our proposed method only requires the EID values. From Table I, we can also find that DirHV-EGO obtains similar or better IGD+ values compared with DirHV-EGO-K on all the test problems. It indicates that our proposed selection metric _h_ ( _·_ ) can also balance the diversity and convergence of the selected query points. The max operation in _h_ ( _·_ ) could promote the diversity of the selected query points. In summary, our proposed method is an effective alternative for batch selection. 

_2) Effectiveness of the Inner Optimizer:_ In each iteration of DirHV-EGO, we need to solve the following _N_ subproblems: 



As discussed earlier, many variants of MOEA/D can be modified to solve these subproblems collaboratively. In this article, MOEA/D-GR is used. To show its effectiveness, we also develop a variant, denoted as DirHV-EGO-DE, which adopts MOEA/D-DE [22] as the inner optimizer. Parameter settings of MOEA/D-DE are the same as [22]. Table II shows the statistics of the IGD+ values of the nondominated solutions obtained by DirHV-EGO-DE and DirHV-EGO. We can find that DirHV-EGO with MOEA/D-GR is better than or at least similar to DirHV-EGO-DE on all the test problems. This is attributed to the global replacement scheme in MOEA/D-GR.

However, to find the most appropriate subproblems, MOEA/D-GR based method requires computing EID(ˆ **_x_** _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_i_</sup> ) for all directions once an offspring **_x_** ˆ is generated. In contrast, MOEA/D-DE based method only requires computing EID(ˆ **_x_** _|_ **_λ_**<sup>_i_</sup> ) in the neighborhood of a subproblem. Thus, the use of MOEA/D-GR results in a higher computational complexity. 

### _C. Performance on Many-objective Optimization Problems_ 

In this section, we compare DirHV-EGO with four state-ofthe-art algorithms, namely, EIM-EGO<sup>1</sup> [24], NSGAIII-EHVI<sup>2</sup> [25], CSEA<sup>1</sup> [26], and K-RVEA<sup>1</sup> [27]. The compared algorithms are all developed for solving expensive many-objective optimization problems (MaOPs), and can be divided into three categories: 

- **MOEGO algorithm** : EIM-EGO is a MOEGO algorithm, which uses an easy-to-compute EI metric to approximate the indicator-based EI. There are three variants of EIMEGO. Here, the hypervolume-based EIM-EGO is used. 

- **GP model assisted evolutionary algorithms** : Both NSGAIII-EHVI and K-RVEA are GP model based surrogate assisted evolutionary algorithms (SAEAs). NSGAIIIEHVI is an extension of NSGA-III [28] and uses EHVI for environmental selection. K-RVEA is based on the RVEA [29] and uses a cluster-based selection method to generate a batch of query points. 

- **Classification model assisted evolutionary algorithm** : CSEA is a classification model based SAEA. It uses classification models to predict the dominance relationship between different solutions, which helps to screen promising solutions from the population. 

- _1) Test Instances:_ As in [24], the DTLZ test suite [30] 

- is used to study the performance of the compared algorithms on MaOPs with four and six objectives, and the number of decision variables is set as 6. 

- _2) General Settings:_ 

- Method to generate the initial samples: Latin hypercube sampling. 

- The maximal number of function evaluations _Ctotal_ : 300. 

- The number of independent runs: 21. 

_3) Other Parameters:_ When dealing with MaOPs, the SLD method is not suitable for decomposition-based algorithms [31]. Alternatively, we use the incremental lattice design (ILD) [31] to generate the weight vectors required in (29). For DirHV-EGO, the number of direction vectors is set as 295 and 462 for _m_ = 4 and _m_ = 6, respectively. The batch size is set as five. Other settings are the same as our previous experimental studies. 

For EIM-EGO, NSGAIII-EHVI, CSEA, and K-RVEA, other parameters use the default settings in PlatEMO or the source codes of [25]. We note that both EIM-EGO and NSGAIIIEHVI can only generate one query point in each iteration. CSEA could generate multiple query points in each iteration, but the number of query points is not controllable. For K- RVEA, the default setting of batch size is five.

_4) Experimental Results:_ Table III presents the statistics of the IGD+ values of the approximation Pareto optimal solutions obtained by different algorithms. The best mean IGD+ values are highlighted by gray background for each problem. To analyze the experimental results, the Wilcoxon rank-sum test at a significance level of 0.05 is used to conduct a significant 

difference analysis. As a result of the Wilcoxon rank-sum test, the symbols +, _−_ , and _≈_ indicate the value obtained by an algorithm is significantly better than, worse than, and competitive with that obtained by DirHV-EGO, respectively. Fig. 7 shows the convergence profiles of different algorithms on all the test instances. 

9 

From the results, we can find that DirHV-EGO performs similarly or better than the others on DTLZ1-3 and DTLZ6. On DTLZ2, the selected MOEGO methods (i.e., DirHV-EGO and EIM-EGO) show significantly better performance than the compared SAEAs (i.e., NSGAIII-EHVI, CSEA, and K- RVEA). However, on the DTLZ4 benchmark, SAEAs can obtain lower mean IDG+ values than MOEGO methods. This disparity can be attributed to the different ways in which MOEGO and SAEA select query points. SAEAs only use the surrogate models for the environmental selection of offspring generated by the population, whereas the MOEGO methods aim to maximize the EI metrics in order to focus the search on regions with a greater likelihood of containing a Pareto optimal objective vector. In certain cases, MOEGO methods may require more observations to improve the accuracy of surrogate models, yet this may not directly result in an improved estimated PF. 

The PF of DTLZ5 and DTLZ6 consist of degenerate curves. It implies that as the number of objectives increases, more direction vectors would become unnecessary, as they are unable to guide the search toward the PF. As shown in Fig. 7, the performance of DirHV-EGO deteriorates on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6 as _m_ increases. However, when _m_ = 4, DirHV-EGO is competitive with other methods on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6. This is likely due to the fact that submodularity-based batch selection in DirHV-EGO can slightly reduce the impact of unnecessary direction vectors. Similar results can also be found on DTLZ7, where the PF is discontinuous. 

### _D. More Discussions_ 

_1) Performance on MOPs with Degenerated PF:_ In multiobjective evolutionary computation, decomposition-based methods often perform not very well on MOPs with degenerated PF, such as DTLZ5 and DTLZ6<sup>3</sup> . Because when direction or weight vectors have no intersection with the true PF, the corresponding subproblems might be unnecessary and result in an inefficient use of computing resources [21]. Fig. 7 shows that the performance of DirHV-EGO also deteriorates on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6 as _m_ increases. However, when _m ≤_ 4, our experimental results (e.g., Fig. 7, Fig. 11 and Fig. 12) show that DirHV-EGO is competitive with the others on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6. 

This is attributed to the two-step selection framework in DirHV-EGO. In the second step (i.e., Algorithm 3), the submodularity-based selection prefers the most valuable candidates and discards the candidates obtained by the unnecessary subproblems. For real-world problems, if the users have some insight into the true PF, they can sample _N_ direction vectors that are not evenly distributed or try to remove the unnecessary subproblems. It could further improve the performance and computational efficiency of DirHV-EGO. 

_2) Computational Time on MaOPs:_ As discussed earlier, the complexity in each iteration of DirHV-EGO is _O_ ( _mn_<sup>3</sup> + ( _mn_<sup>2</sup> + _mN_ ) _Ntmax_ ). Intuitively, the complexity of DirHVEGO is quadratic to parameter _N_ and linear to parameter 

_m_ . However, decomposition-based methods require more direction vectors as the number of objectives increases. In our previous experimental studies, the number of direction vectors _N_ is set as 295 and 462 for _m_ = 4 and _m_ = 6, respectively. As for KB&EHVI-EGO, its complexity in each iteration depends on the complexity of calculating EHVI. Suppose we use the Walking Fish Group (WFG) based algorithm [11] to compute the values of EHVI when _m ≥_ 4. Then, the worst-case complexity in each iteration of KB&EHVI-EGO is _O_ ( _qmn_<sup>3</sup> +( _mn_<sup>2</sup> + _m_ 2<sup>_nk_</sup> ) _qNtmax_ ) with _nk_ denoting the number of nondominated points. Supposing _q_ = 1, KB&EHVIEGO can only be more efficient than DirHV-EGO if the value of _nk_ is lower than log2 _N_ in the worst case<sup>4</sup> . 

### IV. IMPLEMENTATION NOTES FOR MOEA/D-EGO 

In this article, our implementation of MOEA/D-EGO is slightly different from the original MOEA/D-EGO [7] in two aspects: 1) the FuzzyCM is removed, and 2) an adaptive adjustment strategy for **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is used. 

FuzzyCM [7] is an efficient approximation method for GP modeling. It could significantly reduce the computational time when training the GP models. However, MOEA/D-EGO without FuzzyCM could perform better in terms of solution quality [7], [32]. To improve the performance of MOEA/DEGO, we remove the FuzzyCM. Interested readers can find more related discussions in Section VII-E of [7] or Section B.2.2 in the Supplementary File of [32]. 

### _A. Effectiveness of the Adaptive Adjustment Strategy for_ **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> 

In our previous experimental studies, we found that the ETIbased algorithm may converge slowly with a simple setting of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> (e.g., with _zi_<sup>_∗_=</sup><sup>_−_0</sup><sup>_._01forall</sup><sup>_i_).Toimprovetheperfor-</sup> mance of MOEA/D-EGO, we leverage an adaptive adjustment strategy for **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> [33]. Specifically, the reference point _zi_<sup>_∗_isset</sup> as the minimum values of the predicted means obtained by the GP model of _i_ -th objective. To show the effectiveness of this strategy, we develop a variant for MOEA/D-EGO, denoted as Variant-Z. Here, MOEA/D-EGO refers to our implementation, where the FuzzyCM is removed, and the adaptive adjustment strategy for **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is used. For Variant-Z, we set _zi_<sup>_∗_=</sup><sup>_−_0</sup><sup>_._01for</sup> all 1 _≤ i ≤ m_ . Other settings of Variant-Z are the same as MOEA/D-EGO. 

The experimental results of IGD+ values obtained by Variant-Z and MOEA/D-EGO are shown in Table IV. From the results, we can find that Variant-Z and MOEA/D-EGO perform similarly on most test problems except ZDT1-3. For ZDT1 and ZDT2, MOEA/D-EGO significantly outperforms Variant-Z, whereas Variant-Z could obtain a better mean IGD+ value than MOEA/D-EGO on ZDT3. From Fig. 8, we can find that MOEA/D-EGO significantly outperforms Variant-Z on ZDT1, ZDT2, and ZDT6 in terms of the convergence speed. Therefore, the adaptive adjustment strategy we used for **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> is helpful for improving the convergence speed of MOEA/DEGO.

### _B. Comparison of DirHV-EGO-K and MOEA/D-EGO_ 

In each iteration of MOEA/D-EGO, the K-means-based method is used to select _q_ query points. In contrast, DirHVEGO employs a submodular-based greedy selection strategy to achieve this goal. Now, one may care about whether there would be any significant difference in the algorithms’ performance if they utilized the same subset selection method. As in Section IV-B, we developed a variant of DirHV-EGO, named DirHV-EGO-K. It uses the K-means-based method to select query points. The experimental results show that the submodular-based selection strategy is helpful for improving the performance of DirHV-EGO compared with the K-meansbased method. 

In this section, we compare DirHV-EGO-K with MOEA/DEGO with the same setting of batch size (i.e., _q_ = 5). In DirHV-EGO-K, _zi_<sup>_∗_issetas</sup><sup>_−_0</sup><sup>_._01forall1</sup><sup>_≤i ≤m_.Allthe</sup> compared algorithms use the K-means-based selection method to find query points in each iteration. From Table IV, we can find that DirHV-EGO-K performs similarly with MOEA/DEGO on 8 out of 12 test problems. On ZDT1-3 and DTLZ2, both Table IV and Fig. 8 show that DirHV-EGO-K slightly outperforms MOEA/D-EGO. The results suggest that DirHVEGO-K with a simple setting of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> can achieve competitive performance compared to MOEA/D-EGO, which relies on an adaptive adjustment strategy for **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> . Recall that MOEA/DEGO could outperform DirHV-EGO on DTLZ6 in our previous experimental studies. Here, from Table IV, we can find that it is because the adaptive adjustment strategy of **_z_**<sup>_∗_</sup> we used for MOEA/D-EGO does improve its performance.

### V. COMPARISON WITH MULTIPLIED EI 

In Proposition 5, we present an analytical formula for the exact computation of DirHV-EI. This simple formula can be regarded as the product of the single-objective EIs, i.e. (8), of each objective. Actually, this simple but promising idea was first used in the expected improvement matrix (EIM) [24], where the authors aim to develop an easy-tocompute multiobjective EI to approximate the indicator-based EI metrics. Recently, this idea has also been applied to design novel approximation of EHVI [34], [35]. Definitions of these hypervolume-based EI are summarized in Table V. The work most related to DirHV-EI is the multiplied EI (mEI) [35] and its batch versions [36]. In this section, we will discuss the differences between DirHV-EI and mEI from three aspects.

• Target of optimization: f 2 f 2 reference point r ˆ<br>– Utopian point<br>Both mEI [35] and its batch versions [36] aim to target nondominated points<br>a certain part of the PF, rather than attempting to find vectors ξ,ξ <br>a solution set to approximate the whole PF. r ˆ ξ λ vectorattainment surface y<br>– DirHV-EI is designed to find a solution set to approx- I D ( y λ | )<br>imate the whole PF. y y ξ <br>O z *<br>• Improvement function: The computation of all the (a) f 1 (b) f 1<br>hypervolume-based EI criteria presented in Table V is<br><!-- End of picture text -->

- **Improvement function:** The computation of all the hypervolume-based EI criteria presented in Table V is based on the product of single-objective EIs of each objective. One of the primary distinctions between them is how to set the incumbent in (8). 

Fig. 9. Illustrations of the improvement functions (area of the green region) in (a) Multiplied EI and (b) DirHV-EI. In (a), the blue line denotes the IdealNadir line, and the reference point ˆ **_r_** is the closest orthogonal projection of the non-dominated points on the Ideal-Nadir line. In (b), **_λ_** is one of the direction vectors sampled from _P_ **_λ_** . 

- mEI requires an end-user to provide a reference point **_r_** ˆ to define a preferred region. As discussed in Section 2.1 of [36], the user-supplied reference point **_r_** ˆ might be unsuitable. Therefore, the authors proposed to approximate the center of the PF (see Section 2.2 of [36]). Then, as shown in Fig. 9(a), the reference point **_r_** ˆ is set as the closest orthogonal projection of the non-dominated points on the Ideal-Nadir line. 

   - Both m _q_ -EI and _q_ -mEI have two notable shortcomings, which are similar to those of _q_ -EI [20]: 1) the exact computation of EI values is difficult, and approximations, such as the Monte Carlo simulation, have to be used [36]; 2) the dimension of the m _q_ -EI or _q_ -mEI maximization problem is _q × d_ . 

- For DirHV-EI, we sample _N_ direction vectors from _P_ **_λ_** and define _N_ subproblems. As shown in Fig. 9(b), each subproblem measures the hypervolume improvement bounded by an intersection point **_ξ_** . The intersection point **_ξ_** is induced through the modified Tchebycheff scalarization. We note that our proposed improvement function preserves the dominance relationships. For any vector **_y_** _∈_ R<sup>_m_</sup> that belongs to the interior of _U_ , we can always find at least one direction vector **_λ_** _∼ P_ **_λ_** ( _·_ ) such that _ID_ ( **_y_** _|_ **_λ_** ) _>_ 0. 

- DirHV-EGO is designed under the MOEA/D framework and uses a two-step strategy to select _q_ query points in each iteration. The values of DirHV-EI can be analytically computed, and the dimension of the DirHV-EI maximization problem is _d_ .
