IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
1
Hypervolume-Guided Decomposition for Parallel
Expensive Multiobjective Optimization
Liang Zhao and Qingfu Zhang, Fellow, IEEE
Abstract—The hypervolume metric is widely used to guide the
search in multiobjective optimization. However, in parallel expen-
sive multiobjective optimization, the hypervolume-based multi-
point expected improvement (EI) suffers from high computational
overhead and scales poorly with the batch size. To address this
issue, we integrate hypervolume-based EI with the MOEA/D
framework and propose a novel EI, named the expected direction-
based hypervolume improvement (DirHV-EI). The DirHV-EI
only measures the hypervolume improvement within each axis-
parallel box induced by the modified Tchebycheff scalarization.
Thus, it has a simple analytical expression that can be easily
computed. Theoretical analysis indicates that the maximization
of our proposed improvement function can help to maximize
both the weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improve-
ment metrics. Using DirHV-EI, we design a decomposition-
based Bayesian optimization algorithm, called DirHV-EGO, for
solving expensive multiobjective optimization problems. At each
iteration, the MOEA/D is used to maximize the DirHV-EI values
with respect to a number of direction vectors in a collaborative
manner, and a number of candidate solutions can be obtained.
Then, a submodularity-based greedy selection strategy is used to
select multiple query points from the candidates. Experimental
results on both benchmark instances and real-world problems
show that our proposed algorithm is an efficient and effective
method for parallel expensive multiobjective optimization.
Index Terms—Decomposition, efficient global optimization,
expected improvement, expensive multiobjective optimization,
Hypervolume.
I. INTRODUCTION
Bayesian optimization (BO) has been widely accepted as
a mainstream methodology for solving expensive black-box
optimization problems in engineering design [1], [2] and
machine learning [3]. At each iteration, a single-objective BO
method uses some or all of the observed (i.e., evaluated)
samples to build a probabilistic surrogate model, e.g., a
Gaussian process (GP) model, for approximating the objective
function. Then, based on the model, it optimizes an acquisition
function (also known as the infill criterion) to generate one
query point for true function evaluation. A few acquisition
functions have been developed and studied in the context
Manuscript received November 16, 2022; revised February 16, 2023;
accepted March 28, 2023. Date of current version April 4, 2023. This
work was supported by the Hong Kong General Research Fund (11208121,
CityU-9043148), the National Natural Science Foundation of China under
Grant 62276223, and the Key Basic Research Foundation of Shenzhen
(JCYJ20220818100005011). (Corresponding author: Qingfu Zhang)
The authors are with the Department of Computer Science, City Uni-
versity of Hong Kong, Hong Kong, and also with the City University of
Hong Kong Shenzhen Research Institute, Shenzhen, China. (email: liazhao5-
c@my.cityu.edu.hk; qingfu.zhang@cityu.edu.hk)
This article has supplementary material provided by the authors and color
versions of one or more figures available at http://ieeexplore.ieee.org.
of expensive single-objective optimization [4]. Arguably, the
expected improvement (EI) [5] is one of the most popular
acquisition functions [6]. The EI-based BO method is also
known as efficient global optimization (EGO) [7].
Encouraged by the success of EGO for single-objective
optimization, efforts have been made to extend it to expensive
multiobjective optimization [8]–[10]. A critical issue in multi-
objective EGO (MOEGO) methods is how to generalize the
EI function. Expected hypervolume improvement (EHVI) [9]
is one of the most successful multiobjective EI metrics. For
real-life applications, multiple expensive function evaluations,
which rely on the computer or physical simulations, can often
be performed simultaneously on multiple cores or machines.
Consequently, much work has focused on developing parallel
(or batch) MOEGO methods, where q query points are gener-
ated in each iteration [6].
A natural extension of EHVI for parallel MOEGO is the
multi-point EHVI (i.e., q-EHVI) [11]. Using the hypervolume
indicator [12], q-EHVI can measure the potential improvement
when adding q query points simultaneously. However, it suf-
fers from two major difficulties. Firstly, there is no analytical
formula for the exact computation of q-EHVI when q ≥2 [11].
Although Monte Carlo methods can be used, they could be
computationally intensive when many samples are required
for a low estimation error. Secondly, the dimension of the
q-EHVI maximization problem is q × d where d is the di-
mension of the original expensive multiobjective optimization
problem (MOP). This makes maximization of q-EHVI very
difficult when the batch size q increases. Alternatively, one can
sequentially maximize the EHVI for q times to obtain q query
points [11]. But this method could be very time-consuming
when q is large because the computation of EHVI is also
complicated [13]. In this paper, we tackle these two problems
by integrating hypervolume-based EI with decomposition-
based methods.
MOEA/D [14] is a basic evolutionary algorithmic frame-
work for multiobjective optimization. It uses reference vec-
tors (also called weight, direction, or preference vectors) to
decompose a MOP in question into a number of scalarized
functions and optimizes them in a collaborative manner. As
shown in [10], [15], the MOEA/D framework can readily
support parallel MOEGO and scales well with the batch size.
Very recently, theoretical analyses in [16]–[18] suggest that
the hypervolume of a set of objective vectors is equal to the
mean of a scalarized function over a probability distribution of
reference vectors. Therefore, widely used hypervolume-based
multiobjective algorithms and decomposition-based methods
are closely related to each other.
 


<!-- página 4 -->

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
2
Under the MOEA/D framework, we develop a direction-
based hypervolume improvement (denoted as ID) to ensure
that the corresponding EI metric can be computed analyt-
ically. Specifically, our proposed improvement function ID
measures the amount of hypervolume improvement within
an axis-parallel box induced by the modified Tchebycheff
scalarization [19]. In this way, the expectation of ID over
the GP posterior, called expected direction-based hypervolume
improvement (DirHV-EI), can be easily computed through a
simple analytical formula, which is similar to the multiplied
EI (mEI) [20]. Differences between DirHV-EI and mEI are
discussed in the Supplementary File. The major contributions
of our work are summarized as follows1.
• We present DirHV-EI, a novel hypervolume-based EI that
can be easily integrated into the MOEA/D framework.
• We investigate the relationships between ID and the other
two representative improvement functions. Theoretical
analysis indicates that the maximization of ID can help
to maximize both the weighted hypervolume and the
Tchebycheff improvement metrics.
• We design a decomposition-based MOEGO algorithm,
called DirHV-EGO, which uses a submodular-based
greedy selection strategy to select q query points from
the candidate set.
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section
II introduces the related background. Section III details the
DirHV-EI metric. Section IV presents the DirHV-EGO algo-
rithm. Section V conducts numerical experiments to test the
proposed method. Finally, section VI concludes the paper.
II. BACKGROUND
A. Expensive Multiobjective Optimization
A continuous MOP can be defined as
minimize
F(x) = (f1(x), . . . , fm(x))T
subject to
x ∈Ω⊆Rd
(1)
where F : Ω7→Rm consists m objective functions, x is the
decision variable, fi(x) is the i-th objective function, Rm is
the objective space, and Ω⊆Rd is the decision space. We
assume all the objective functions are continuous, noise-free,
but expensive to evaluate.
Let u = (u1, . . . , um)T , v = (v1, . . . , vm)T be two vectors
in the objective space. u is said to dominate v, denotes as
u ≺v, if and only if ui ≤vi for all i = 1, . . . , m and uj < vj
for at least one index j. u is said to weakly dominate v,
denotes as u ⪯v, if and only if ui ≤vi for all i = 1, . . . , m.
u is said to strictly dominate v, denotes as u < v, if and only
if ui < vi for all i = 1, . . . , m. x∗∈Ωis Pareto optimal
if there is no x ∈Ωsuch that F(x) ≺F(x∗). x∗∈Ωis
weakly Pareto optimal if there does not exist another x ∈Ω
such that F(x) strictly dominates F(x∗). The Pareto set (PS)
is the set of all Pareto optimal points. The Pareto front (PF)
is the image of PS in the objective space.
1The code is available at https://github.com/mobo-d/DirHV-EGO
B. Parallel Multiobjective Efficient Global Optimization
Existing parallel MOEGO methods can be roughly classified
into the following three categories according to their acquisi-
tion functions:
1) Parallel MOEGO Based on Single-objective EI: To
directly use the single-objective EI, a simple and efficient
strategy is to randomly transform the MOP into a scalarized
function in each iteration and build a GP model to approxi-
mate the scalarized function. ParEGO [8] is representative of
this category. Such approaches can be extended for parallel
MOEGO by randomly creating q scalarized functions in each
iteration [21]. However, it could be challenging to directly
build GP models for each scalarized function when the value
of q is large [22].
2) Decomposition-based Parallel MOEGO: The methods
in this category adopt the decomposition strategy [14] and
build GP models for each objective but not the scalarized
functions. Depending on the decomposition methods (e.g.,
scalarization functions) used, different multiobjective EI met-
rics such as expected Tchebycheff improvement (ETI) [10] and
expected improvement of penalty-based boundary intersection
(EPBII) [23] have been proposed and studied. A representative
algorithm in this category is MOEA/D-EGO [10]. In each
iteration, it maximizes the ETI values with respect to a number
of subproblems and could generate a set of candidates. Then,
q query points can be easily selected from the candidates.
However, there is no simple analytical expression for these
EI metrics, and approximation methods have to be used for
computing them [10], [23].
3) Indicator-based Parallel MOEGO: Using the indicator
metrics in multiobjective optimization, many multiobjective
EI metrics have been proposed, such as hypervolume-based
EI (i.e., EHVI) [9], Euclidean distance-based EI [24], and
maximin distance-based EI [25]. Recently, some of them have
been extended for parallel MOEGO, such as q-EHVI [11].
In each iteration, the maximization of q-EHVI could directly
generate q query points. However, it suffers from high compu-
tational overhead. Alternatively, Zhan et al. [26], [27] proposed
an easy-to-compute metric to approximate the indicator-based
EI and extended it to parallel optimization. Yang et al. [28]
suggested dividing the bi-objective space into multiple sub-
spaces, with multiple query points generated by maximizing
the truncated EHVI [29] in each sub-space. Additionally,
Gaudrie et al. [30] proposed the q-mEI metric to rapidly reach
a target point on the PF.
Algorithm 1 outlines the general structure of paral-
lel MOEGO methods that use the multiobjective EI (i.e.,
decomposition-based and indicator-based EI). In Line 1, some
initial samples {xi}C0
i=1 are generated with a Design of Exper-
iment (DOE) method, such as the Latin hypercube sampling.
After that, a parallel MOEGO method follows a sequential
decision-making framework. In each iteration, q query points
are selected and evaluated through four main steps: objective
space normalization (if necessary), modeling, EI-based selec-
tion, and parallel evaluation of true objective vectors. These
four components are repeated until the computational budget
is exhausted.

1)
Hypervolume: Let A = {F(x1), . . . , F(xn)} be the
set of all the evaluated objective vectors in D and r =

of the set A relative to r is defined as [12]
H(A, r) = Volume{v ∈Rm|∃a ∈A : a ≺v ≺r}.

Both hypervolume and scalarization function play an im-
portant role in multiobjective optimization. The following
theorem implies the hypervolume metric defined in (2) can
be expressed as the mean of a scalarized function over a
probability distribution of reference vectors [16]–[18].
Theorem 1 (Hypervolume scalarization [16]–[18]). The hy-
pervolume of a set of objective vectors A with respect to
a reference point r is equivalent to the expectation of a
scalarized function over a probability distribution of reference
vectors:
H(A, r) = Ew∼Pw(·)

2) A Unified Definition of Multiobjective EI: Suppose D =

As illustrated in Fig. 1(b), the improvement function IH(y)
actually measures how much the hypervolume would increase
when adding y to A. The Expected Hypervolume Improve-
ment (EHVI) of x [9] is:
EIH(x) = Ey∼p(y|x,D)[IH(y)].
(7)
When m = 2 or 3, EHVI can be exactly computed with
time complexity of Θ(nk log nk) [32], [33], where nk is the
number of nondominated points in A. However, when m > 3,
existing methods for computing (7) are complicated and time-
consuming because it is NP-hard in m [13], [34].
Let X = {x(1), . . . , x(q)} ⊂Ωbe the set of any q points.
To measure the improvement when adding F(X) to A, IH
can be extended as:
IH(F(X)) = H(A ∪{F(X)}, r) −H(A, r).
(8)
Let p(F(X)|D) be the joint posterior distribution over
F(X). q-EHVI [11] is defined as:
q-EIH(X) = EF (X)∼p(F (X)|D)[IH(F(X))].
(9)
We note that there is no known analytical formula for
the exact computation of q-EHVI when q ≥2 [11], [35].
Moreover, X is an arbitrary subset of Ωof size q, rendering the
q-EHVI maximization complicated to solve when q is large.
An alternative is to use the sequential greedy approximation
methods, such as the Kriging Believer (KB) [36]. As shown
in Table I, such approximation conditions the EHVI on the
previously selected query points and sequentially maximizes
the EHVI for q times to get q query points [11].

III. DIRECTION-BASED HYPERVOLUME IMPROVEMENT
AND ITS PROPERTIES
In this section, we provide a new hypervolume-based EI
based on the MOEA/D framework. We also present the
connections of our proposed method with the weighted hy-
pervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement metrics.
A. EHVI within an Axis-parallel Box
The exact computation of EHVI is complicated when the
nondominated region U is irregular and high-dimensional.
However, the values of EHVI within an axis-parallel box can
be exactly and efficiently computed for problems with any
number of objectives [34]. The following lemma provides a
special case when the axis-parallel box is only bounded by an
upper bound point.
Lemma 1. Let u∗= (u∗
1, . . . , u∗

where Φ(·) and ϕ(·) are the standard Gaussian cumulative
distribution function (cdf) and probability density function
(pdf), respectively.
Note that the RHS of (10) is similar to the product of single-
objective EIs, which can be easily computed. This property
has been used for constructing effective approximations of
EHVI [20], [26], [37]. Recently, it has also been extended
for parallel MOEGO [27], [30]. However, these hypervolume-
based EI metrics did not consider the connection between
hypervolume and decomposition-based methods. To generate q
query points in each iteration, they either need to sequentially
maximize a single-point EI for q times [11], [27] or maximize
a multi-point EI in Rm×q [30]. It’s inefficient if the batch size
q is large.
Recall that the parallel MOEGO methods based on the
MOEA/D framework scale well as q increases [10], [15]. It is
natural to study whether it is feasible to integrate hypervolume-
based EI with the MOEA/D framework. Clearly, Theorem 1,
Lemma 1, and the geometric property of modified Tchebycheff
scalarization [19] can inspire us to achieve this goal.

B. Direction-based Hypervolume Improvement
As discussed earlier, there are two components in a multi-
objective EI (4), the posterior distribution p(y|x, D) and
the improvement function I(y). The posterior distribution is
usually the same for different multiobjective EI metrics. Then,
the key is how to define the improvement function. Here, we
use the MOEA/D framework to define a new improvement
function that is a special case of IH(y|u∗) in Lemma 1.
1) Definition: As in Section II-C, suppose that A is the set
of all the evaluated objective vectors in D. The attainment
surface induced by A, denoted as AS, is defined as the
boundary of the set {v ∈Rm|∃a ∈A : a ⪯v} [38].
λ = (λ1, . . . , λm)T is called a direction vector in this paper
if ∥λ∥2 = 1 and λi > 0 for all i = 1, . . . , m. Let z∗=

As illustrated in Fig. 2, the attainment surface can be
regarded as an upper bound of the PF. In other words, the
true PF must be below the attainment surface. Thus, ξ is an
upper estimation of the intersection of the true PF and the ray
starting from z∗with direction λ.
Definition 1 (Direction-based hypervolume improvement).

2) Basic Properties of ID: Fig. 2(a) illustrates the geomet-

given direction vector λ, ID(y|λ) is the volume of the axis-
parallel box bounded by ξ and y when y ≺ξ. As shown in
Fig. 2(b), we usually use multiple direction vectors at the same

and r, i.e., [z∗, r] := {u ∈Rm|z∗⪯u ⪯r}. Pλ is defined
as a positive probability measure on the following set:

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
5
Considering all possible direction vectors sampled from Pλ,
we would like to make the following comments:
• When m = 1, the computation of ID(y|λ) essentially
reduces to that of the standard single-objective improve-
ment function.
• If y /∈A is strictly dominated by r and is not dominated
by any points in A, there is at least one direction vector
λ satisfying ID(y|λ) > 0 and vice versa.
• ID(y|λ) is monotonic with respect to Pareto dominance.
Let y1, y2 /∈A be two different objective vectors that
are strictly dominated by r and are not dominated by any
points in A. If y1 ≺y2, we always have ID(y1|λ) ≥
ID(y2|λ) for any direction vector, and there exists at
least one direction vector λ∗such that ID(y1|λ∗) >
ID(y2|λ∗), e.g., λ∗∈arg maxλ ID(y2|λ).
3) Maximization of ID and Pareto Optimality:
Proposition 1. For a direction vector λ. If x∗is the optimal
solution to
max
x∈ΩID(F(x)|λ)
(13)
and ID(F(x∗)|λ) > 0. Then x∗is Pareto optimal.
Proposition 2. Suppose x∗∈Ωis Pareto optimal to (1),
F(x∗) /∈A. Then there exists at least one direction vector λ
such that ID(F(x∗)|λ) > 0.
If the attainment surface induced by A does not include the
whole PF, these two properties imply that we can always find
a direction λ and improve A by maximizing (12). Therefore,
our proposed improvement function ID can be used as a
scalarization function to deal with nonconvex PFs, whereas
optimization of the weighted sum scalarization may not lead
to any improvement when the PF is not convex [40, Sec. 4.7.4].

Contour plots of (a) normalized IH(y), and (b) normalized IAD(y)

As shown in Fig. 3, IAD(y) is different from the standard
hypervolume improvement IH(y). Due to the weight function
P(u ≺ξ(λ)), IAD prefers the vectors that could dominate
more intersection points ξ induced by λ ∼Pλ. Proposition 3
implies that if we maximize IAD(y) in (15), then the weighted
hypervolume improvement defined in (16) also achieves its
maximum. From Definition 2, for a direction vector λ sampled
from Pλ, ID(y|λ) can be regarded as an unbiased estimation
of IAD(y). It implies that if an algorithm maximizes ID(y|λ)
for a randomly selected λ at each iteration, its behavior may
be similar to those algorithms for maximizing the weighted
hypervolume improvement IAD(y).
For multiobjective optimization, there are usually an infinite
number of Pareto optimal solutions. Thus, it is impossible to
use a single solution y∗to maximize the (weighted) hypervol-
ume metric H(·, r) (see Fig. 3). To obtain multiple promising
solutions, we can sample N direction vectors, denoted as
λ1, . . . , λN, that are uniformly distributed in the objective
space (see Fig. 2). Then, N subproblems can be defined and
each one concentrates on locating a promising solution in the
subregion Uλi:
max
x∈ΩID(F(x)|λi), 1 ≤i ≤N.
(17)

E. Expected Direction-based Hypervolume Improvement
As in Section II-C, assume that each objective fi(x) can be
modeled by a GP model and these objectives are independent
of one another. For x ∈Ω, denote the posterior distribution
over y = F(x) as p(y|x, D). Then, the expected direction-
based hypervolume improvement (DirHV-EI) of x with respect
to A, i.e., the mean of ID(y|λ) over p(y|x, D), is:
EID(x|λ) = Ey∼p(y|x,D)[ID(y|λ)].
(22)
Applying ξ and ID(y|λ) to Lemma 1, we have:
Proposition 5. Let τi = ξi−ˆµi(x)
ˆσi(x) . Then
EID(x|λ) =
m
Y
i=1
ˆσi(x)(τiΦ(τi) + ϕ(τi))
(23)
where Φ(·) and ϕ(·) are the standard Gaussian cdf and pdf,
respectively.
This proposition gives a computationally efficient way to
compute EID(x|λ). Moreover, we can prove:
Proposition 6. Let x1, x2 ∈Ωbe two unevaluated points,
ˆµ(xi) = (ˆµ1(xi), . . . , ˆµm(xi))T ,
and
ˆσ(xi) = (ˆσ1(xi), . . . , ˆσm(xi))T .
Then
1) when ˆσ(x1) = ˆσ(x2), ˆµ(x1) ≺ˆµ(x2), EID(x1|λ) >
EID(x2|λ), and
2) when ˆµ(x1) = ˆµ(x2), ˆσ(x1) ≻ˆσ(x2), EID(x1|λ) >
EID(x2|λ).
This proposition indicates that with the same uncertain level,
EID is consistent with Pareto dominance; and if two different
points have the same expected objective values, EID favors
one with higher uncertainty. It is desirable that an expected
improvement function have this monotonicity property [26].
IV. PROPOSED ALGORITHM USING EID
A. Algorithm
Our proposed algorithm, named DirHV-EGO, adopts a
similar framework as Algorithm 1 except for Line 5, where a
two-step method is used to select q query points. It works as
follows:
1) Q ←{arg max
x∈Ω
EID(x|λi), 1 ≤i ≤N} (Algorithm 2).
2) Select q query points from Q (Algorithm 3).
In step 1, we use N direction vectors to define N subprob-
lems, which are extensions of (17) for expensive optimization:
max
x∈ΩEID(x|λi), 1 ≤i ≤N.
(24)
Then, we use the MOEA/D framework to solve these subprob-
lems collaboratively, rather than solving them independently
by single-objective algorithms. It is because these subprob-
lems are related to one another. When two direction vectors
are close, the optimal solutions of their subproblems should
be similar. This similarity among subproblems allows us to
leverage the neighborhood structure in MOEA/D and solve
(24) in a single run. In contrast, independently solving all the
subproblems in (24) ignores the similarity among subproblems
and is less efficient.
In step 2, we propose a submodular-based greedy selection
strategy to select q query points from the candidate set Q,
which is a finite set. It is noted that directly maximizing the
multi-point EHVI requires finding q optimal points in the high-
dimensional decision space, i.e., Rq×d. In contrast, selecting
q points from the finite set Q should be easier.
For many real-world problems, each objective can have a
different range, and objective space normalization is often
required. In this article, a simple normalization method [26]
is used in Line 3 of Algorithm 1. For each i ∈{1, . . . , m},
the i-th normalized objective value of any point x ∈D
is obtained through (fi(x) −zmin
i
)/(zmax
i
−zmin
i
), where
zmin
i
= min
x∈D fi(x) and zmax
i
= max
x∈D fi(x).
B. Using MOEA/D to maximize EID
MOEA/D [14] is a basic algorithmic framework in multiob-
jective evolutionary computation. Over the past few decades,
many multiobjective algorithms have been developed using the
MOEA/D framework [43], [44]. Some of them can be easily
extended to optimize (24) in a single run. In this section, we
take MOEA/D-GR [45] as an example to maximize the EID
criterion for finding the candidate set Q. The main steps are
summarized in Algorithm 2.
Our approach requires N direction vectors λ1, . . . , λN
sampled from Pλ. In Line 1, we firstly generate N weight
vectors w1, . . . , wN using the simplex-lattice design [46] or
incremental lattice design [47]. Then, for each weight vector
w = (w1, . . . , wm)T , we can construct a direction vector
λ = (λ1, . . . , λm)T through
λi =
ri ∗wi −z∗
i

i )2 ,
(25)
where z∗
i = −0.01 and ri = 1.1 since we normalize each
objective into [0, 1] interval.
 


<!-- página 9 -->

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
7
Algorithm 2 Using MOEA/D-GR to Maximize the DirHV-EI
Input:
• N: population size (i.e., the number of subproblems);
• z∗: Utopian point;
• Tm: mating neighborhood size;
• Tr: replacement neighborhood size;
• δ: the probability that parent solutions are selected from
the neighborhood;
• tmax: maximum generation.
Output: final population.
Step 1: Initialization
1: generate N direction vectors λ1, . . . , λN.
2: generate initial population {˜x1, . . . , ˜xN} randomly and
evaluate the corresponding posterior means and variances.
3: calculate the value of EID(˜xi|λi) for each i = 1, . . . , N.
4: initialize the Tm-neighborhood and Tr-neighborhood of
each individual ˜xi, i = 1, . . . , N.
Step 2: Evolution
5: for i = {1, 2, . . . , N} do
6:
mating selection: select two indexes m1 and m2 from
the Tm-neighborhood of ˜xi with the probability δ or
the whole population with the probability 1 −δ.
7:
reproduction and repair: get a new solution ˆx from
˜xi, ˜xm1, ˜xm2 using reproduction and repair operators.
8:
evaluate the posterior mean and variance at point ˆx.
9:
global replacement: find the most suitable subprob-
lem j for ˆx according to (26). For each current solution
˜xk in the Tr-neighborhood of ˜xj, replace ˜xk by ˆx if
EID(ˆx|λk) > EID(˜xk|λk).
10: end for
Step 3: Stopping criteria
11: If the stopping conditions are satisfied, then discard the
duplicate individuals and output the final population; oth-
erwise, go to Step 2.
In Line 2, N individuals {˜x1, . . . , ˜xN} are randomly gen-
erated. For i = 1, . . . , N, ˜xi is regarded as the current
solution for the i-th subproblem. The Tm-neighborhood and
Tr-neighborhood of ˜xi are the set of Tm closest and Tr closest
subproblems to the i-th subproblem in terms of the Euclidean
distances among direction vectors, respectively [45].
In Line 9, the global replacement scheme proposed in
MOEA/D-GR is used, which can avoid the mismatch between
solutions and subproblems. For each subproblem at each
generation, suppose ˆx ∈Ωis a newly generated solution
through the crossover, mutation, and repair operators. We use
EID to select the most appropriate subproblem j for ˆx:
j = arg max
1≤i≤N EID(ˆx|λi).
(26)
For all the index k in the Tr-neighborhood of subproblem j,
if EID(ˆx|λk) > EID(˜xk|λk), we replace ˜xk with ˆx. Using
this strategy, one high-quality solution could update multiple
subproblems in Line 9 and accelerate the convergence of
the algorithm. Meanwhile, each ˆx cannot update too many
subproblems because Tr is often much smaller than N. It helps
maintain the diversity of the population.
C. Submodularity-based Batch Selection
MOEA/D-EGO adopts an ad-hoc method to select a few
solutions for true objective function evaluations from a set of
candidates. We propose to formulate the selection task as a
submodular maximization problem.
As in Section II-C, let X be the set of q points. We can
generalize ID and IAD to measure the improvement when
adding F(X) to A as follows:
ID(F(X)|λ) =

(27)
IAD(F(X)) = Eλ∼Pλ(·)[ID(F(X)|λ)].
(28)
Clearly, IAD(F(X)) is a Pareto compliant indicator [41].
However, it is very difficult, if not impossible, to have an
analytical and efficient way for computing the expectation of
ID(F(X)|λ) where F(X) are random.
To facilitate the computation in the batch selection,

of

we
use

lower

adding X to D along the direction λ. Considering the ran-
domness of fi(x) and multiple direction vectors, we measure
the contribution of X to D in DirHV-EGO by:
h(X) = 1

The above set function h(X) is a monotone submodular

is NP-hard in general. The greedy algorithm is commonly
used. It provides a (1 −1/e)-approximation guarantee when
the submodular function is monotone [49]. In Algorithm 3, we
present an implementation of the greedy algorithm for (30).
The algorithm initializes X = ∅. At each iteration, it adds to
X the following element:

V. EXPERIMENTAL STUDIES
In this section, we make the following comparisons to
empirically study the performance of our proposed method.
For a fair comparison, all the compared methods use the same
framework shown in Algorithm 1, except for Line 5.
1) To show the effectiveness of DirHV-EI, we first set
q = 1 and compare our proposed method against two
representative multiobjective EI criteria, namely, EHVI2
[9], and ETI3 [10].
2) The major motivation for proposing EID is to facilitate
hypervolume-based parallel MOEGO. Three related par-
allel MOEGOs, including KB&EHVI-EGO [11], PEIM-
EGO [27], and MOEA/D-EGO [10], are then selected for
comparison under different settings of batch size q.
A. Experiment Settings
1) Test Instances: In our experimental studies, three test
suites are used, including the ZDT test suite [50], DTLZ test
suite [51], and five real-world MOPs [52]. Settings of the
number of objectives and the number of decision variables
for each test problem are shown in Table II.

metrics are used to measure the algorithm performance.
Inverted generational distance plus (IGD+): Let P be the
obtained approximate PF and P ∗be a set of well-distributed
objective vectors sampled from PF. The IGD+ is defined as

Hypervolume: The IGD+ metric requires a set of reference
vectors P ∗, which is unavailable when the true PF is unknown.
Therefore, the hypervolume metric is used for performance
assessment when dealing with real-world problems. As rec-
ommended in [52], we calculate H(P, r) in the normalized
objective space [0, 1]m and the reference point r is set as
(1.1, . . . , 1.1)T . A larger hypervolume value means that the
set P is a better approximation for the PF in terms of both
convergence and diversity.
3) General settings:
• Number of initial samples C0: 11d −1.
• Method to generate the initial samples: optimal Latin
hypercube sampling [59].
• Maximal number of function evaluations Ctotal: 200 for
m = 2 and 300 for m = 3.
• Number of independent runs: 21.
4) Parameter settings of GP model: To have a fair compar-
ison, the DACE toolbox [60] is used to build the GP model
for each algorithm. The related parameters are set as follows.
• The regression function: regpoly0.
• The correlation function c(x, x′): corrgauss.
• Range of each hyperparameter θi: [10−6, 102].
5) Inner optimizer: The differential evolution (DE) algo-
rithm and MOEA/D-GR are used as the inner optimizers to
maximize the EI functions. Detailed settings are as follows.
• Population size: The population size N is set as 200 for
m = 2 and 210 for m = 3, and the initial population is
randomly generated.
• Termination condition: The maximum number of gener-
ations is set as 50.
• Reproduction operators: The DE operators (F = 0.5,
CR = 1) and polynomial mutation (Index = 20, pm =
1/d) are used for offspring generation [61].
• Other control parameters for MOEA/D-GR: As in [45],
we set Tm = Tr = 0.1 × N and δ = 0.8.
All the algorithms are coded based on the MATLAB plat-
form PlatEMO [62]. To analyze the experimental results, the
Wilcoxon rank-sum test at a significance level of 0.05 is used
to conduct a significant difference analysis. As a result of the
Wilcoxon rank-sum test, the symbols +, −, and ≈indicate the
value obtained by a method is significantly better than, worse
than, and competitive with that obtained by DirHV-EI based
method, respectively.
B. Effectiveness of DirHV-EI
In this section, we set q = 1 and conducted a set of
numerical experiments to study the performance of four EI
metrics, including EHVI, ADirHV-EI, ETI, and DirHV-EI.
ADirHV-EI is defined as the expectation of IAD(y) over
the posterior p(y|x, D). By Proposition 3, ADirHV-EI is a
weighted EHVI with γ(u) = P(u ≺ξ(λ)), whereas EHVI
can be regarded as a special weighted EHVI with γ(u) = 1.
Given N direction vectors sampled from Pλ, Definition 2 tells
us ADirHV-EI can be approximated by DirHV-EI:

We first compare ADirHV-EI with EHVI. Both of them are
maximized by the DE algorithm in each iteration. Table III
presents the statistics of the IGD+ values obtained by different
EI metrics. From the results, we can observe that ADirHV-
EI, i.e. the mean of DirHV-EI with respect to Pλ, performs
similarly to EHVI on 7 out of 12 test problems. On ZDT1-
3, DTLZ2, and DTLZ6, ADirHV-EI performs slightly better
than EHVI. Therefore, the empirical results align with our
statements in Proposition 3. Besides, it indicates that when the
computational budget is limited, the performance of EHVI can
be improved with a carefully designed weight function γ(u).
Then, we compare our proposed DirHV-EI, i.e. E[ID(y|λ)],
and ETI, i.e. E[IT (y|λ)], under the MOEA/D framework.
In each iteration, we sample N direction vectors to define
N subproblems as in (24). To make the comparison fair,
the subproblems defined by both DirHV-EI and ETI are
maximized through MOEA/D-GR, and the individual with the
highest EI values in the final population is selected as the
query point. Other settings are the same.
Table III shows that DirHV-EI can achieve mean IGD+
values that are comparable to or even lower than those attained
by ETI. Besides, Fig. 4 shows that DirHV-EI converges faster
than ETI on ZDT1 and ZDT2. A major reason is that the
values of IT (y|λ) are directly related to z∗(see Fig. 1 in the
Supplementary File). When z∗is inaccurately estimated, the
ETI based algorithm may converge slowly. By contrast, the
values of ID(y|λ) mainly rely on the intersection points ξ
and may be less affected by z∗.
Table III also indicates that DirHV-EI using the MOEA/D
framework outperforms ADirHV-EI using the DE algorithm on
many test problems. This suggests that a multiobjective algo-
rithm may be more effective for optimizing the multiobjective
EI than a single-objective algorithm.

C. Experimental Results on ZDT and DTLZ
This section compares DirHV-EGO with the following
three parallel MOEGO algorithms, i.e., KB&EHVI-EGO [11],
PEIM-EGO [27], and MOEA/D-EGO [10]. The corresponding
multiobjective EI criteria are listed as follows.
• Kriging Believer and EHVI (KB&EHVI): KB&EHVI is a
sequential greedy approximation to q-EHVI. As shown in
Table I, it conditions the EHVI on the previously selected
query points and sequentially maximizes the EHVI for q
times to get q query points.
• Pseudo Expected Improvement Matrix (PEIM): The
PEIM metric is defined as the product of an influence
function and the expected improvement matrix [26]. By
sequentially maximizing the PEIM metric for q times in
each iteration, one can get the q query points without
updating the GP models. There are three variants of
PEIM. Here, the hypervolume-based PEIM is used.
• Expected Tchebycheff improvement (ETI): ETI is also
designed under the MOEA/D framework. Since it may
converge slowly with a fixed setting of z∗, we adaptively
update z∗when maximizing ETI [63]. Besides, the
FuzzyCM used in the original MOEA/D-EGO is removed
to improve its performance [21].
Table IV presents the mean IGD+ values of the approxima-
tion Pareto optimal solutions obtained by the four algorithms.
From the results, we can find that DirHV-EGO obtains the best
mean IGD+ values on ZDT1-2 and DTLZ1-2 with different
settings of batch size. On ZDT3, DTLZ3, and DTLZ5, DirHV-
EGO obtains better or similar mean IGD+ values compared
with others with different settings of q. Fig. 5 shows the
convergence profiles of different algorithms in terms of the
log IGD+ values on all the test problems when q = 5. We can
find that DirHV-EGO significantly outperforms the others on
ZDT1-3, DTLZ1-3, and DTLZ5.

The mean IGD+ values obtained by all the algorithms on
ZDT4 are greater than 30. It indicates that ZDT4 is difficult to
solve with a limited budget [10]. When q = 1, Table III shows
that the decomposition-based methods could obtain lower
mean IGD+ values than EHVI-EGO on ZDT4. However, Table
IV shows that KB&EHVI-EGO and PEIM-EGO outperform
DirHV-EGO and MOEA/D-EGO when q ≥5. It is because the
f2 of ZDT4 is difficult to optimize and should be minimized
first with more budget. But DirHV-EGO and MOEA/D-EGO
waste computational resources for maintaining the diversity of
query points when the batch size is large.
On ZDT6 and DTLZ4, KB&EHVI-EGO gets the best mean
IGD+ values, whereas DirHV-EGO performs similarly on most
test instances except on ZDT6 with q = 10. On DTLZ6,
MOEA/D-EGO performs the best in terms of the IGD+
metric, followed by DirHV-EGO. But Table III shows that
DirHV-EI could get lower mean IGD+ values than ETI on
DTLZ6 when we use a fixed setting of z∗. It indicates the
adaptive adjustment strategy of z∗we used for MOEA/D-EGO
does improve its performance. On DTLZ7, MOEA/D-EGO
outperforms the others when q = 2 and performs similarly
with KB&EHVI-EGO and DirHV-EGO when q ≥5.
In summary, we can conclude that our proposed method
is competitive compared with the three parallel MOEGO
methods with different settings of batch size.
D.
Discussion on Running Time
1)
Analysis of the Computational Complexity: In each
iteration of DirHV-EGO, the computational overhead mainly
 


<!-- página 13 -->

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
11
consists of three parts: training the GP models, maximizing the
EI metrics, and selecting q query points from candidates. For
training the GP models for all the objectives, the computational
complexity is O(mn3) with n denoting the number of obser-
vations. When maximizing the DirHV-EI under the MOEA/D
framework, the computational overhead mainly includes the
time for calculating ˆµi(x), ˆσi(x), and EID(x|λ). Once the
GP models have been constructed, each prediction requires
O(n) for ˆµi(x) and O(n2) for ˆσi(x) [9]. As for EID(x|λ),
Proposition 5 provides a simple formula with a complexity of
O(m). Then, it requires O(mN) to find the most appropri-
ate subproblem, i.e., (26), in each iteration of Algorithm 2.
Therefore, the computational complexity for maximizing the
EI metrics is O((mn2+mN)Ntmax). When selecting q points
from N candidates, it requires O(mN 2) to compute the values
of EID(x|λj) for all x ∈Q and 1 ≤j ≤N. Then, it requires
O(N) in each iteration of Algorithm 3. Therefore, selecting q
query points from candidates runs in O(mN 2 +qN). Assum-
ing q < N, the complexity in each iteration of DirHV-EGO is
O(mn3 +(mn2 +mN)Ntmax). Intuitively, the complexity of
DirHV-EGO is cubic to parameter n, quadratic to parameter
N, and linear to parameters m and tmax.
2)
Comparison of Computational Time: This section em-
pirically studies the running time of DirHV-EGO and three
other parallel MOEGO algorithms with respect to different
settings of batch size. The number of EGO iterations is set as
50 for different algorithms. Assume the true function evalua-
tions of q query points obtained in each iteration are performed
in parallel, e.g., in q machines. Then, we mainly focus on
the computational times of the algorithm itself. All the exper-
iments are conducted on the same hardware platform (CPU:
i7-9700, Memory: 64GB). The experimental results on DTLZ2
are shown in Fig. 6. The results show that the running times for
both KB&EHVI-EGO and PEIM-EGO increase rapidly with
increasing q. In contrast, the CPU times for DirHV-EGO and
MOEA/D-EGO increase much more slowly. It demonstrates
the computational advantage of decomposition-based methods
for parallel expensive multiobjective optimization.
When m = 3, the time complexity in each iteration of
KB&EHVI-EGO is O(qmn3 + (mn2 + nk log nk)qNtmax)
with nk denoting the number of nondominated points. When
q = 1, mN is much larger than nk log nk in our experimental
settings. Then, KB&EHVI-EGO could be more efficient than
DirHV-EGO. It is consistent with the experimental results
shown in Fig. 6. However, when q > 3, DirHV-EGO outper-
forms KB&EHVI-EGO in terms of CPU time. When q = 15,
the mean computational time is 9066 seconds (about 2.5
hours) for KB&EHVI-EGO, but only 818 seconds (about 13.6
minutes) for DirHV-EGO. In this case, DirHV-EGO is 11
times faster than KB&EHVI-EGO. PEIM-EGO uses a similar
framework to KB&EHVI-EGO. When m = 3, they perform
similarly in terms of CPU time.
From Fig. 6, we can also find that DirHV-EGO is more
efficient than MOEA/D-EGO. There are two reasons: i) the
ETI approximation is more complicated than the computation
of DirHV-EI, and ii) MOEA/D-EGO needs to adjust z∗
adaptively at each iteration, whereas DirHV-EGO can work
well on most test problems with a simple setting of z∗.

The mean running time of four EI-based parallel MOEGO methods
on DTLZ2 with m = 3, d = 6, Ctotal = 65+50q over 10 independent runs.
E. Experimental Results on Real-world Problems
In this section, DirHV-EGO is further tested on five real-
world MOPs. The batch size is set as 5, and other settings
are the same as in Section V-A. The statistics of hypervolume
values are summarized in Table V. It can be seen that DirHV-
EGO significantly outperforms the other algorithms on RE1-4.
On RE5, EHVI-EGO performs the best, followed by DirHV-
EGO. Fig. 7 plots the final solutions obtained in the run with
the median hypervolume value of each algorithm on RE5.
With a limited computational budget, DirHV-EGO does not
approximate the upper right corner of PF very well compared
with KB&EHVI-EGO. But it could find more nondominated
solutions in the upper left corner of PF.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION, VOL. XX, NO. X, APRIL 2023
12
VI. CONCLUSION
This paper has introduced expected direction-based hyper-
volume improvement (DirHV-EI) that integrates hypervolume-
based EI with the MOEA/D framework. The proposed DirHV-
EI can be analytically computed with the complexity of
O(m). Theoretical analysis indicates that maximization of
our proposed metric could also serve to maximize both the
weighted hypervolume and the Tchebycheff improvement met-
rics. To solve expensive MOPs, we have designed an algorithm
called DirHV-EGO. At each iteration, it maximizes the values
of DirHV-EI with respect to a number of direction vectors
simultaneously. Then, a submodularity-based batch selection
strategy is proposed to select multiple query points for parallel
computing.
Empirical results demonstrate that DirHV-EGO is an effi-
cient and effective method for parallel expensive multiobjec-
tive optimization. However, there are still some limitations.
• DirHV-EGO requires more direction vectors as the num-
ber of objectives increases in order to better explore
the search space. But its computational complexity is
quadratic to the number of direction vectors, which
indicates DirHV-EGO may be inefficient when there are
too many objectives.
• DirHV-EGO uses N uniformly distributed direction vec-
tors, which may not be the best setting for MOPs with
irregular PF.
• A continuous MOP usually has an infinite number of
Pareto optimal solutions. But DirHV-EGO can only find
a limited number of optimal solutions to approximate the
set of all the optimal solutions.
• DirHV-EGO uses GP models to approximate the objec-
tives. Due to the fact that GP models are prone to suffer
from the curse of dimensionality, it can be a challenge
for DirHV-EGO to deal with high-dimensional MOPs.
Apart from applying DirHV-EGO to real-life problems,
there are several interesting research topics for future work:
• Extensions to other complex multiobjective scenarios [6],
such as constrained, multi-fidelity, and noisy.
• Integration of DirHV-EGO with reference vector adjust-
ment methods [44].
• DirHV-EGO with Pareto set learning [64], which has been
shown to be able to identify a low-dimensional manifold
to approximate the entire PS.
• Integration of DirHV-EI with other surrogate models,
such as probabilistic classifier [65].

Liang Zhao is a Ph.D. student in the Department
of Computer Science at the City University of Hong
Kong, Hong Kong. He received his B.Sc. and M.Sc.
degrees from the School of Marine Science and
Technology at Northwestern Polytechnical Univer-
sity in Xi’an, China, in 2017 and 2020, respectively.
His research interests include evolutionary multi-
objective optimization, Bayesian optimization, and
machine learning.
Qingfu Zhang (Fellow, IEEE) received the B.Sc.
degree in mathematics from Shanxi University,
Taiyuan, China, in 1984, and the M.Sc. degree in
applied mathematics and the Ph.D. degree in infor-
mation engineering from Xidian University, Xi’an,
China, in 1991 and 1994, respectively. He is a Chair
Professor at the Department of Computer Science,
City University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong. His
main research interests include evolutionary com-
putation, optimization, machine learning, and their
applications. Prof. Zhang has been a highly cited
researcher in computer science since 2016. He is an Associate Editor of the
IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON EVOLUTIONARY COMPUTATION and IEEE
TRANSACTIONS ON CYBERNETICS.

Supplementary Material: Hypervolume-Guided
Decomposition for Parallel Expensive
Multiobjective Optimization
Liang Zhao and Qingfu Zhang, Fellow, IEEE
Abstract—This document is the supplementary material to the
article entitled “Hypervolume-Guided Decomposition for Parallel
Expensive Multiobjective Optimization”. This document includes
six sections. In Section I, we present preliminary techniques
used in this study. Section II provides detailed proofs of Lemma
1 and Propositions 3-6. Section III offers additional empirical
analysis of the performance of DirHV-EGO. In Section IV,
we present technical implementation details of MOEA/D-EGO.
Section V delves into a comparison between DirHV-EI and
mEI, highlighting their respective differences. Finally, Section
VI includes additional experimental results.
I. PRELIMINARY TECHNIQUES
A. Gaussian Process
Gaussian Process (GP) model [1], also known as Kriging
model [2], [3], is a widely used nonparametric approach for
modeling a black-box function y = f(x) for x ∈Rd. It
treats f(x) as a random variable and makes the following
assumptions:
• For any x ∈Rd, f(x) is a sample of the following
Gaussian random variable:
µ0 + ϵ(x)

ϵ(x) and ϵ(x′), depends only on x −x′. A commonly
used form of the correlation function is:
c(x, x′) = exp

B. Single-objective Expected Improvement
Suppose that we want to minimize f(x), where x ∈Ω.
Let D = {xi, f(xi)}n
i=1 be n observed samples. For any
unevaluated point x, the posterior distribution over y = f(x)
can be denoted as p(y|x, D). The improvement function of y
is defined with respect to the incumbent ξ:
I(y) = [ξ −y]+,
(6)
where [·]+ denotes the max{·, 0} operation, and ξ is often
set as the minimum of observed function values, i.e., ξ =
min{f(x1), . . . , f(xn)}.
The expectation of improvement (EI) of x [5] is defined as:
EI(x) = Ey∼p(y|x,D)[I(y)].
(7)
Suppose GP model is used to model f(x). Then, the poste-
rior distribution p(y|x, D) obeys (3). Applying integration by
parts, (7) can be simplified as (details are available at [6])
EI(x; ξ) = ˆσ(x)[τΦ (τ) + ϕ (τ)],
(8)
where τ =
ξ−ˆµ(x)
ˆσ(x) ; ˆµ(x) and ˆσ(x) are given in (4) and
(5), respectively; Φ(·) and ϕ(·) are the standard Gaussian
cumulative distribution function (cdf) and probability density
function (pdf), respectively.

Fig. 1.
Illustrations of (a) direction-based hypervolume improvement
ID(y|λ) (area of the green region), and (b) Tchebycheff improvement
IT (y|λ) (length of the red line).
As illustrated in Fig. 1, (19) implies that IT (y|λ) measures
the distance between ξ and ξ′ when y ≺ξ. ξ′ is the
intersection point of the attainment surface and the ray starting
from z∗with direction λ when adding y to A. Consequently,
the values of IT (y|λ) are highly impacted by z∗. Thus,
the determination of z∗is essential for the maximization of
IT (y|λ). When z∗is inaccurately estimated, the ETI based
algorithm may converge slowly. By contrast, the values of
ID(y|λ) mainly rely on the intersection points ξ and may
be less affected by z∗.
The geometric property of IT (y|λ) also indicates that
IT (y|λ) > 0 if and only if ID(y|λ) > 0. Therefore,
the probability of improvement (PI) criteria [10] induced by
IT (y|λ) and ID(y|λ) are equivalent, i.e., P(IT (y|λ) > 0) =
P(ID(y|λ) > 0) when y are random. However, their expecta-
tions, i.e. E[IT (y|λ)] and E[ID(y|λ)], are not equivalent since
the values of IT (y|λ) and ID(y|λ) are different for the same
y and λ.

<!-- página 20 -->

4
3) Comments on the Weighted Hypervolume Improvement:
The hypervolume indicator is widely used in multiobjective
optimization. A major reason is that it is compliant with the
Pareto dominance [15]. The weighted (or generalized) hyper-
volume indicator [16] is a variant of the standard hypervolume
indicator. When the weight function γ : Rm →R+ is a
positive function, the weighted hypervolume indicator is also
compliant with the Pareto dominance [16], [17]. This indicator
can also be used to define a weighted hypervolume improve-
ment for expensive multiobjective optimization [18]. To ensure
that the weight function remains positive, researchers typically
resort to defining the weight function through probability
density functions (pdfs) [17]–[19]. But the resulting weighted
hypervolume metrics are usually more difficult to compute
than the standard hypervolume indicator [15].
Proposition 3 tells us IAD is also a weighted hypervolume
improvement metric with γ(u) = P(u ≺ξ(λ)). The weight
function γ(u) = P(u ≺ξ(λ)) is strictly positive for any u
in the interior of U. Therefore, it also has desirable properties
like other (weighted) hypervolume improvement metrics. For
instance, IAD is compliant with the Pareto dominance for the
objective vectors in the nondominated region U.

III. FURTHER ANALYSIS OF DIRHV-EGO
This section is divided into four parts.
1) Section III-A empirically analyzes the sensitivity of
DirHV-EGO to the Utopian point z∗and batch size q.
2) Section III-B discusses the effectiveness of the major
components in DirHV-EGO.
3) Section III-C explores the performance of DirHV-EGO
on many-objective optimization problems (MaOPs).
4) Section III-D contains more discussions on the perfor-
mance of DirHV-EGO.

Sensitivity to the Utopian Point z∗: To investigate the
impacts of z∗on the performance of DirHV-EGO, some repre-
sentative values (e.g., −1, −0.1, −0.01, −0.0001, −0.000001
and 0) of z∗
i are employed to test on the ZDT and DTLZ
test instances. The experimental results are shown in Fig. 5.

2) Sensitivity to the Number of Batch Size q: When the
total budget Ctotal is fixed, a single-objective parallel EGO al-
gorithm is reported to get a poorer performance as q increases
for different problems [20]. However, it is not always true for
expensive multiobjective optimization. Fig. 6 shows that the
performance of DirHV-EGO on several test problems, such as
ZDT3 and DTLZ6, does get worse when q increases, likely
due to the decrease in the number of iterations required to
update the GP models, resulting in poorer approximations of
the objectives.
However, on DTLZ2 and DTLZ5, DirHV-EGO could per-
form slightly better with a larger q. On ZDT1 and ZDT6,
DirHV-EGO performs best when the batch size is five. On
DTLZ3, DirHV-EGO obtains the lowest mean log IGD+ value
when q = 8. It indicates that adding multiple query points in
each iteration can be better than sequentially adding a number
of samples (i.e., q = 1) for some expensive MOPs. This is
because a multiobjective optimization algorithm should take
both convergence and diversity into consideration. It could be
helpful if the estimated PF can be improved from different
regions by adding a diversity of samples simultaneously.
In summary, there is no best choice of batch size for
different expensive black-box MOPs, and the commonly-used
setting, i.e., q = 5, is acceptable for most problems.
B.
Effectiveness of Major Components in DirHV-EGO
1) Effectiveness of the Proposed Batch Selection Strategy:
To study the effectiveness of the proposed submodularity-
based batch selection strategy, we develop two variants of
DirHV-EGO, denoted as DirHV-EGO-R and DirHV-EGO-K.
These two variants differ from DirHV-EGO only in how to
select multiple query points from the candidate set Q (i.e.,
Algorithm 3). Specifically, DirHV-EGO-R randomly selects q
query points in each iteration, whereas DirHV-EGO-K uses
the K-means-based selection method [7]. We note that the
candidates whose EID values are zeros should be removed
from Q before using the random selection or the K-means
algorithm. Otherwise, low-quality candidates may be selected.
The comparison results are shown in Table I.
From the results on ZDT4, DTLZ1, DTLZ3, and DTLZ4,
we can find that DirHV-EGO performs similarly to DirHV-
EGO-R. Probably, the quality of each point in Q is similar
when the test problems are difficult to solve. For the other test
problems, both DirHV-EGO-K and DirHV-EGO outperform
the variant using random selection. This is because some
subproblems might be unnecessary [21], and a proper batch
selection method is usually required.
The K-means-based method uses the distances between the
decision variables and EI values of candidates to balance the

attributed to the global replacement scheme in MOEA/D-GR.

C.
Performance on Many-objective Optimization Problems
In this section, we compare DirHV-EGO with four state-of-
the-art algorithms, namely, EIM-EGO1 [24], NSGAIII-EHVI2
[25], CSEA1 [26], and K-RVEA1 [27]. The compared algo-
rithms are all developed for solving expensive many-objective
optimization problems (MaOPs), and can be divided into three
categories:
• MOEGO algorithm: EIM-EGO is a MOEGO algorithm,
which uses an easy-to-compute EI metric to approximate
the indicator-based EI. There are three variants of EIM-
EGO. Here, the hypervolume-based EIM-EGO is used.
• GP model assisted evolutionary algorithms: Both
NSGAIII-EHVI and K-RVEA are GP model based surro-
gate assisted evolutionary algorithms (SAEAs). NSGAIII-
EHVI is an extension of NSGA-III [28] and uses EHVI
for environmental selection. K-RVEA is based on the
RVEA [29] and uses a cluster-based selection method to
generate a batch of query points.
• Classification model assisted evolutionary algorithm:
CSEA is a classification model based SAEA. It uses
classification models to predict the dominance relation-
ship between different solutions, which helps to screen
promising solutions from the population.
1)
Test Instances: As in [24], the DTLZ test suite [30]
is used to study the performance of the compared algorithms
on MaOPs with four and six objectives, and the number of
decision variables is set as 6.
2)
General Settings:
• Method to generate the initial samples: Latin hypercube
sampling.
• The maximal number of function evaluations Ctotal: 300.
• The number of independent runs: 21.
3)
Other Parameters: When dealing with MaOPs, the
SLD method is not suitable for decomposition-based algo-
rithms [31]. Alternatively, we use the incremental lattice
design (ILD) [31] to generate the weight vectors required in
(29). For DirHV-EGO, the number of direction vectors is set
as 295 and 462 for m = 4 and m = 6, respectively. The batch
size is set as five. Other settings are the same as our previous
experimental studies.
For EIM-EGO, NSGAIII-EHVI, CSEA, and K-RVEA, other
parameters use the default settings in PlatEMO or the source
codes of [25]. We note that both EIM-EGO and NSGAIII-
EHVI can only generate one query point in each iteration.
CSEA could generate multiple query points in each iteration,
but the number of query points is not controllable. For K-
RVEA, the default setting of batch size is five.
1We leverage existing implementations from the PlatEMO platform [23].
The codes are available at https://github.com/BIMK/PlatEMO.
2The codes are available at https://github.com/Pang-Yong/NSGAIII-EHVI.

4) Experimental Results: Table III presents the statistics of
the IGD+ values of the approximation Pareto optimal solutions
obtained by different algorithms. The best mean IGD+ values
are highlighted by gray background for each problem. To
analyze the experimental results, the Wilcoxon rank-sum test
at a significance level of 0.05 is used to conduct a significant
difference analysis. As a result of the Wilcoxon rank-sum
test, the symbols +, −, and ≈indicate the value obtained
by an algorithm is significantly better than, worse than, and
competitive with that obtained by DirHV-EGO, respectively.
Fig. 7 shows the convergence profiles of different algorithms
on all the test instances.


<!-- página 25 -->

9
From the results, we can find that DirHV-EGO performs
similarly or better than the others on DTLZ1-3 and DTLZ6.
On DTLZ2, the selected MOEGO methods (i.e., DirHV-EGO
and EIM-EGO) show significantly better performance than
the compared SAEAs (i.e., NSGAIII-EHVI, CSEA, and K-
RVEA). However, on the DTLZ4 benchmark, SAEAs can
obtain lower mean IDG+ values than MOEGO methods. This
disparity can be attributed to the different ways in which
MOEGO and SAEA select query points. SAEAs only use the
surrogate models for the environmental selection of offspring
generated by the population, whereas the MOEGO methods
aim to maximize the EI metrics in order to focus the search
on regions with a greater likelihood of containing a Pareto
optimal objective vector. In certain cases, MOEGO methods
may require more observations to improve the accuracy of
surrogate models, yet this may not directly result in an
improved estimated PF.
The PF of DTLZ5 and DTLZ6 consist of degenerate curves.
It implies that as the number of objectives increases, more
direction vectors would become unnecessary, as they are
unable to guide the search toward the PF. As shown in Fig. 7,
the performance of DirHV-EGO deteriorates on DTLZ5 and
DTLZ6 as m increases. However, when m = 4, DirHV-EGO
is competitive with other methods on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6.
This is likely due to the fact that submodularity-based batch
selection in DirHV-EGO can slightly reduce the impact of
unnecessary direction vectors. Similar results can also be
found on DTLZ7, where the PF is discontinuous.
D.
More Discussions
1)
Performance on MOPs with Degenerated PF:
In
multiobjective evolutionary computation, decomposition-based
methods often perform not very well on MOPs with de-
generated PF, such as DTLZ5 and DTLZ63. Because when
direction or weight vectors have no intersection with the true
PF, the corresponding subproblems might be unnecessary and
result in an inefficient use of computing resources [21]. Fig. 7
shows that the performance of DirHV-EGO also deteriorates
on DTLZ5 and DTLZ6 as m increases. However, when m ≤4,
our experimental results (e.g., Fig. 7, Fig. 11 and Fig. 12) show
that DirHV-EGO is competitive with the others on DTLZ5 and
DTLZ6.
This is attributed to the two-step selection framework in
DirHV-EGO. In the second step (i.e., Algorithm 3), the
submodularity-based selection prefers the most valuable candi-
dates and discards the candidates obtained by the unnecessary
subproblems. For real-world problems, if the users have some
insight into the true PF, they can sample N direction vectors
that are not evenly distributed or try to remove the unnecessary
subproblems. It could further improve the performance and
computational efficiency of DirHV-EGO.
2)
Computational Time on MaOPs: As discussed earlier,
the complexity in each iteration of DirHV-EGO is O(mn3 +
(mn2 + mN)Ntmax). Intuitively, the complexity of DirHV-
EGO is quadratic to parameter N and linear to parameter
3The PF of three-objective DTLZ5 or DTLZ6 is a 2-D curve.
m. However, decomposition-based methods require more di-
rection vectors as the number of objectives increases. In our
previous experimental studies, the number of direction vectors
N is set as 295 and 462 for m = 4 and m = 6, respectively.
As for KB&EHVI-EGO, its complexity in each iteration
depends on the complexity of calculating EHVI. Suppose we
use the Walking Fish Group (WFG) based algorithm [11]
to compute the values of EHVI when m ≥4. Then, the
worst-case complexity in each iteration of KB&EHVI-EGO is
O(qmn3+(mn2+m2nk)qNtmax) with nk denoting the num-
ber of nondominated points. Supposing q = 1, KB&EHVI-
EGO can only be more efficient than DirHV-EGO if the value
of nk is lower than log2 N in the worst case4.
IV. IMPLEMENTATION NOTES FOR MOEA/D-EGO
In this article, our implementation of MOEA/D-EGO is
slightly different from the original MOEA/D-EGO [7] in two
aspects: 1) the FuzzyCM is removed, and 2) an adaptive
adjustment strategy for z∗is used.
FuzzyCM [7] is an efficient approximation method for
GP modeling. It could significantly reduce the computational
time when training the GP models. However, MOEA/D-EGO
without FuzzyCM could perform better in terms of solution
quality [7], [32]. To improve the performance of MOEA/D-
EGO, we remove the FuzzyCM. Interested readers can find
more related discussions in Section VII-E of [7] or Section
B.2.2 in the Supplementary File of [32].
A.
Effectiveness of the Adaptive Adjustment Strategy for z∗
In our previous experimental studies, we found that the ETI-
based algorithm may converge slowly with a simple setting of
z∗(e.g., with z∗
i = −0.01 for all i). To improve the perfor-
mance of MOEA/D-EGO, we leverage an adaptive adjustment
strategy for z∗[33]. Specifically, the reference point z∗
i is set
as the minimum values of the predicted means obtained by the
GP model of i-th objective. To show the effectiveness of this
strategy, we develop a variant for MOEA/D-EGO, denoted as
Variant-Z. Here, MOEA/D-EGO refers to our implementation,
where the FuzzyCM is removed, and the adaptive adjustment
strategy for z∗is used. For Variant-Z, we set z∗
i = −0.01 for
all 1 ≤i ≤m. Other settings of Variant-Z are the same as
MOEA/D-EGO.
The experimental results of IGD+ values obtained by
Variant-Z and MOEA/D-EGO are shown in Table IV. From
the results, we can find that Variant-Z and MOEA/D-EGO
perform similarly on most test problems except ZDT1-3. For
ZDT1 and ZDT2, MOEA/D-EGO significantly outperforms
Variant-Z, whereas Variant-Z could obtain a better mean IGD+
value than MOEA/D-EGO on ZDT3. From Fig. 8, we can
find that MOEA/D-EGO significantly outperforms Variant-Z
on ZDT1, ZDT2, and ZDT6 in terms of the convergence speed.
Therefore, the adaptive adjustment strategy we used for z∗is
helpful for improving the convergence speed of MOEA/D-
EGO.

B.
Comparison of DirHV-EGO-K and MOEA/D-EGO
In each iteration of MOEA/D-EGO, the K-means-based
method is used to select q query points. In contrast, DirHV-
EGO employs a submodular-based greedy selection strategy
to achieve this goal. Now, one may care about whether
there would be any significant difference in the algorithms’
performance if they utilized the same subset selection method.
As in Section IV-B, we developed a variant of DirHV-EGO,
named DirHV-EGO-K. It uses the K-means-based method to
select query points. The experimental results show that the
submodular-based selection strategy is helpful for improving
the performance of DirHV-EGO compared with the K-means-
based method.
In this section, we compare DirHV-EGO-K with MOEA/D-
EGO with the same setting of batch size (i.e., q = 5). In
DirHV-EGO-K, z∗
i is set as −0.01 for all 1 ≤i ≤m. All the
compared algorithms use the K-means-based selection method
to find query points in each iteration. From Table IV, we can
find that DirHV-EGO-K performs similarly with MOEA/D-
EGO on 8 out of 12 test problems. On ZDT1-3 and DTLZ2,
both Table IV and Fig. 8 show that DirHV-EGO-K slightly
outperforms MOEA/D-EGO. The results suggest that DirHV-
EGO-K with a simple setting of z∗can achieve competitive
performance compared to MOEA/D-EGO, which relies on an
adaptive adjustment strategy for z∗. Recall that MOEA/D-
EGO could outperform DirHV-EGO on DTLZ6 in our pre-
vious experimental studies. Here, from Table IV, we can find
that it is because the adaptive adjustment strategy of z∗we
used for MOEA/D-EGO does improve its performance.

V. COMPARISON WITH MULTIPLIED EI
In Proposition 5, we present an analytical formula for the
exact computation of DirHV-EI. This simple formula can
be regarded as the product of the single-objective EIs, i.e.
(8), of each objective. Actually, this simple but promising
idea was first used in the expected improvement matrix
(EIM) [24], where the authors aim to develop an easy-to-
compute multiobjective EI to approximate the indicator-based
EI metrics. Recently, this idea has also been applied to design
novel approximation of EHVI [34], [35]. Definitions of these
hypervolume-based EI are summarized in Table V. The work
most related to DirHV-EI is the multiplied EI (mEI) [35] and
its batch versions [36]. In this section, we will discuss the
differences between DirHV-EI and mEI from three aspects.

• Target of optimization:
– Both mEI [35] and its batch versions [36] aim to target
a certain part of the PF, rather than attempting to find
a solution set to approximate the whole PF.
– DirHV-EI is designed to find a solution set to approx-
imate the whole PF.
• Improvement function: The computation of all the
hypervolume-based EI criteria presented in Table V is
based on the product of single-objective EIs of each
objective. One of the primary distinctions between them
is how to set the incumbent in (8).
– mEI requires an end-user to provide a reference point
ˆr to define a preferred region. As discussed in Sec-
tion 2.1 of [36], the user-supplied reference point ˆr
might be unsuitable. Therefore, the authors proposed
to approximate the center of the PF (see Section 2.2
of [36]). Then, as shown in Fig. 9(a), the reference
point ˆr is set as the closest orthogonal projection of
the non-dominated points on the Ideal-Nadir line.
– For DirHV-EI, we sample N direction vectors from Pλ
and define N subproblems. As shown in Fig. 9(b), each
subproblem measures the hypervolume improvement
bounded by an intersection point ξ. The intersection
point ξ is induced through the modified Tchebycheff
scalarization. We note that our proposed improvement
function preserves the dominance relationships. For
any vector y ∈Rm that belongs to the interior of
U, we can always find at least one direction vector
λ ∼Pλ(·) such that ID(y|λ) > 0.
• Batch version: In [36], the authors proposed two batch
versions of mEI, named multiplicative q-EI (mq-EI) and
q-mEI. The definitions of mq-EI and q-mEI are shown
below.

Illustrations of the improvement functions (area of the green region)
in (a) Multiplied EI and (b) DirHV-EI. In (a), the blue line denotes the Ideal-

– Both mq-EI and q-mEI have two notable shortcomings,
which are similar to those of q-EI [20]: 1) the exact
computation of EI values is difficult, and approxima-
tions, such as the Monte Carlo simulation, have to be
used [36]; 2) the dimension of the mq-EI or q-mEI
maximization problem is q × d.
– DirHV-EGO is designed under the MOEA/D frame-
work and uses a two-step strategy to select q query
points in each iteration. The values of DirHV-EI can
be analytically computed, and the dimension of the
DirHV-EI maximization problem is d.
VI.
ADDITIONAL EXPERIMENTAL RESULTS
Figs. 10-13 shows the convergence profiles in terms of the
log IGD+ or hypervolume value in each algorithm across
all selected test suites. Figs. 14-22 plots the final solutions
obtained in the run with the mean IGD+ or mean hypervolume
value of each algorithm for each test instance.
